                         Preface

This training circular provides the gunnery training plan for all prerequisite and live-fire
training for the M1A2, Abrams heavy tank. It includes all training events through crew
qualification following the integrated weapons training strategy structure.
TC 3-20.31-120 is written using a plan, prepare, execute and assess methodology. This
publication applies to all Abrams variants.

The principal audience for TC 3-20.31-120 is commanders, trainers, and Master
Gunners of movement and maneuver units. Trainers and educators throughout the Army
will also use this publication.

Commanders, staffs, and subordinates ensure that their decisions and actions comply
with applicable United States, international, and, in some cases host-nation laws and
regulations. Commanders at all levels ensure their Service members operate in
accordance with the law of war and the rules of engagement. (See FM 6-27 for legal
compliance.)

The use of a trade or brand name does not constitute endorsement of any specific
commercial product, commodity, service, or enterprise by the U.S. Army.

This publication applies to the Army’s active component and reserve component unless
otherwise stated. Users and readers of this publication are invited to submit
recommendations that will improve its effectiveness.

The proponent of TC 3-20.31-120 is the United States Maneuver Center of Excellence.
The preparing agency is the Department of Tactics, Training, and Doctrine, United
States Army Maneuver Center of Excellence. Send comments and recommendations on
DA Form 2028 (Recommended Changes to Publications and Blank Forms) to
Commander, United States Army Maneuver Center of Excellence and Fort Benning,
ATZK-TDD (TC 3-20.31.120), Fort Benning, GA 31905-5410; by email to
usarmy.benning.mcoe.mbx.doctrine@army.mil; or submit an electronic DA Form 2028.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  xiii

                      Introduction

TC 3-20.31-120 provides the details of the integrated weapons training strategy (IWTS)
structure specifically designed for U.S. Army heavy tanks. This training circular
provides the training strategy for maneuver commanders to train, evaluate, and assess
their unit’s overall proficiency at home station. The heavy tank training strategy includes
only the critical training events to build crew platform readiness using the IWTS’s
standard training structure.

The heavy tank training strategy maximizes the training aids, devices, simulators,
simulations (TADSS), and live-fire events capabilities in a systematic manner to
increase and sustain Soldier and crew proficiency, effectiveness, and lethality. The
training strategy is capability-based (Soldier, crew, ammunition, optics/sensors, and
weapon system) and maximizes the training resources available across the force.

The heavy tank training strategy encompasses all critical training that builds a unit’s
ability to shoot, move, and communicate effectively and efficiently. Although units
conduct other training as part of their training plan, the critical training path includes
only those events that are conducted to ensure the maximum experience is achieved or
gained by the trained Soldier and crew.

The terms tank, or heavy tank, are applicable to all variants of the M1A2 Abrams series
main battle tank within this publication for simplicity. This publication categorizes tanks
in three classes based on their chassis, armor protection, primary armament, and weight
as:

     • Heavy tank—a tracked armored vehicle with a 105 millimeter (mm) or larger
          cannon as its primary armament, and a gross weight over 60 tons.
           Its primary purpose is to defeat all types of ground threats including heavy
                tanks, as well as rotary wing aircraft.
           It operates as part of an Armor, mechanized, or heavy Cavalry
                company/troop formation.

     • Medium tank—a tracked armored vehicle with a 90mm or larger cannon as its
          primary armament, with a gross weight between 35 and 60 tons.
           Its primary purpose is to defeat all types of ground threats including light
                and heavy tanks, as well as rotary wing aircraft.
           It operates tactically in an Infantry support role with fortification and
                armor defeating capabilities.

     • Light tank—a tracked armored vehicle with a 75mm to 105mm cannon as its
          primary armament, with a gross weight less than 35 tons.
           Its primary purpose is to defeat all types of ground threats including light
                and medium armored wheeled and tracked vehicles, as well as dismounted
                threats.
           It operates tactically in an Infantry support role with light-armor and
                fortification defeating capabilities.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  xv


---

Introduction

     The heavy tank training strategy has six training events (called tables) that culminate in
     their demonstration of proficiency during a live-fire qualification event. Heavy tank
     training consists of all the training events required to provide the commander with an
     effective demonstration of crew capabilities, employment skill, and lethality to build
     unit readiness.

     Units should maximize their time and resources by designing their heavy tank training
     plans such that crews train through proficiency to support the section, platoon, and
     company, whether as an organic small unit, an attachment, or a cross-attachment. This
     level of synchronization will enable the unit to transition to advanced, higher echelon
     collective training seamlessly. Introductory figure 1 shows the structure for the heavy
     tank training strategy. The training strategy described within this publication follows the
     standard training structure found in TC 3-20.0 and synchronizes with the training
     principles found in FM 7-0.

     Introductory figure 1. Heavy tank training strategy overview

xvi  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                                         Introduction

HEAVY TANK TABLES

     Heavy tanks follow the standard six-table format of the integrated weapons training
     strategy through live-fire qualification (proficiency). The six heavy tank tables are listed
     below and shown in introductory figure 2 for context—

          • Table I–Gunnery Skills Test (GST).
          • Table II–Situational Training Exercise—Virtual (STX-V).
          • Table III–Situational Training Exercise (STX).
          • Table IV–Basic.
          • Table V–Practice.
          • Table VI–Qualification.

Introductory figure 2. Heavy tank tables with linkage to higher echelon
                              collective training, example

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  xvii


---

Introduction

TRAINING STRATEGY DESIGN

     It is important for units to follow the training strategy as designed. Tanks are complex
     platforms with state-of-the-art fire control systems, ballistic computers, thermal optics,
     target hand-off capabilities, hunter/killer functionality, with a high level of mobility. The
     munitions employed are purpose-based and provide extended stand-off distances that
     directly impact crew survivability and mission accomplishment on the battlefield.

     Engagements are designed to increase the speed of the crew’s direct fire kill chain to
     defeat tactical dilemmas. All training and standards are based on achieving the desired
     effects against the tactical dilemma before those threats deliver lethal fires. Training to
     this standard requires a training model that instructs and evaluates the crew’s ability to
     employ their platform, fire control system, optics, munitions, and redundant systems
     against the variety to target packages they are expected to defeat.

     The training model identifies the general capabilities of the crew, system, optics,
     ammunition, most probably threat arrays, and battlefield conditions to determine the
     maximum number of engagements a trained crew should experience. The training model
     includes the maximum number of engagements possible based on the basic conditions:

          • Day or night limited visibility.
          • Offense or defense operations.
          • Precision or degraded engagement skills.
          • Threat array:

                 Number of threats—one to four within presentation.
                 Posture of threats—stationary or moving.
                 Range band—lose (less than 300 meters), mid (300 to 900 meters), long

                     (900 meters to 1,600 meters), or extended (more than 1,600 meters).
          • Chemical environment.

     Without considering the possible ammunition types and two separate firers, or the ability
     to engage simultaneous targets with multiple weapon systems, the training model
     baseline includes over 10,000 engagement possibilities. Within those engagements,
     approximately 1,920 engagements are designed for training within precision gunnery
     simulations per fiscal year by a crew. Simulations are critical to the education and
     experience of the crew.

     To augment the training strategy, the unit needs experienced tank commanders and
     gunners who serve as subject matter experts, have trained and certified trainers, Master
     Gunner, instructor/operators, vehicle crew evaluators (VCEs). Providing training
     resources alone is not enough. Without certified trainers and leaders, units will struggle
     to achieve the fundamentals of tactical employment and experience difficulty achieving
     trained, qualified, and lethal crews.

     Training is designed to teach the crews to accurately engage and destroy enemy targets
     with the appropriate weapon system to build confidence between crew members and in
     the platform through varying conditions. Units are required to follow the training
     strategy without deviation unless authorized by the brigade combat team commander.

xviii  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025

     shown in introductory figure 3. This example is the general model in use for tank
     platforms.

Introductory figure 3. Training strategy structure based on crew experience

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  xix


---

Introduction

     Those engagements provide the bulk of crew experience. Within those 1,920
     engagements in simulation, they include a variety of the conditions based on their skill
     and ability to progress through the simulation’s training model.

           SIMULATIONS PROVIDE 95 PERCENT OF CREW
                         ENGAGEMENT EXPERIENCE

          Within simulations, with the varying number of engagements, and
          the varying number of targets presented, the training plan includes
          over 2,000 trigger squeezes for main gun engagements and an
          additional 1,000 for the coaxial machine gun. The main gun trigger
          squeezes provided in simulation equate to approximately 20 years
          of live-fire ammunition authorizations per crew (99.5 main gun
          rounds per year).

     The live-fire training strategy includes 33 live fire tasks per gunnery through crew
     qualification. For an active component crew, firing twice per year, and additional
     collective live fire engagements, the total number of engagements presented for live-fire
     experience is approximate 80 engagements total.

xx  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                                         Introduction

FAILING TO TRAIN ENGAGEMENTS IMPACT READINESS

     Units that fail to provide sufficient simulations training time for their crews and fail to
     execute the live-fire events to standard hinder their ability to tactically employ their
     platform. An example of common reductions in the training strategy across a fiscal year
     is shown in introductory figure 4 for comparison.

Introductory figure 4. Negative impacts to crew experience based on reduced
                                                  training

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  xxi


---

Introduction

     Units that have a robust simulations training plan and follow the training strategy
     provide 2,000 engagements to their crews. When units cannot provide the training to the
     crews per the training strategy, the impacts to the crew’s experience can be described
     using the number of engagements fired.

     Introductory figure 4 on page xxi shows the impacts from the training strategy’s 2,000
     engagement designed structure. Common instance in the unit’s training calendar causes
     the crew’s experience on engagements to drop to 727 engagements total.

     A crew that loses over 60 percent of the training strategy’s programmed engagement
     experience may provide a unit a qualified crew initially. Crews that are not provided the
     training resources by their unit will not fully develop their engagement skills and
     lethality. In subsequent live-fire events, crew evaluations will identify basic failures in
     the conduct of fire, reticle aim, fire control system management, and engagement
     techniques. Those training gaps will ultimately result in failed qualification attempts.

     When units remove the training events from their training plan, including the use and
     management of simulations, the proficiency and lethality of the unit diminishes rapidly.
     In those instances, the commander loses qualified crews, unit readiness, and the
     flexibility to correct talent management or personnel turbulence issues rapidly.

xxii  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                              Introduction

CHAPTER STRUCTURE

     Chapters 1 through 6 in this training circular provide an overview of the training event,
     the training event definition, the purpose, method, and end state of the training, and a
     guide to plan, prepare, execute, and assess each event. Chapter 7 details methods to
     manage the proficiency of the crew once they achieve proficiency.

CHAPTER 1–TABLE I, GUNNERY SKILLS TEST

     Chapter 1 describes the training and evaluation requirements for each Soldier’s GST. It
     includes required training tasks for crew protection such as roll-over drills, crew
     evacuation from all positions, and react to multiple types of vehicle fires. The chapter
     provides general information for the unit to plan, prepare, execute, and assess the event
     using the 8-step training model.

CHAPTER 2–TABLE II, SITUATIONAL TRAINING EXERCISE–VIRTUAL

     Chapter 2 describes the section-level STX-V. The chapter includes the purpose, method,
     and end state of the event based on the simulation system’s training model and the
     required gate to live fire (GTLF) exercise. The chapter provides general information for
     the unit to plan, prepare, execute, and assess the event using the 8-step training model.

CHAPTER 3–TABLE III, SITUATIONAL TRAINING EXERCISE

     Chapter 3 describes the section-level STX using various TADSS and includes the
     purpose, method, and end state of the event based on the platform’s capabilities and
     crew’s tasks. The chapter provides general information for the unit to plan, prepare, and
     execute, the event using the 8-step training model. Chapter 3 provides required training
     tasks for crew protection such as berm drills (defilade to enfilade movements within
     defensive firing positions), roll-over drills, crew evacuation from all positions, and react
     to multiple types of vehicle fires.

CHAPTER 4–TABLE IV, BASIC

     Chapter 4 describes the live-fire task requirements for screening or zeroing the main gun
     and zeroing the coaxial machine gun and commander’s machine gun prior to conducting
     further live-fire training. It continues by defining the training requirements for ten
     defensive live-fire engagements for the tank commander, gunner, or simultaneous
     execution against single and multiple stationary or moving threats.

CHAPTER 5–TABLE V, PRACTICE

     Chapter 5 provides the detailed engagement requirements for the 10 live-fire
     engagements on the practice course of fire. It provides advanced and complex
     engagements built upon all previous training. The engagements are specifically designed
     to provide increased challenges to the crew using 3/4 scale targets at full range, complex
     target arrays, and high tempo changes to the fire control system to best prepare crews
     for their qualification event.

CHAPTER 6–TABLE VI, QUALIFICATION

     Chapter 6 describes the engagement requirements for live-fire qualification for heavy
     tank crews. These engagements are designed to ensure the crew can effectively engage

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  xxiii


---

Introduction

     and defeat common threat arrays expected in a tactical environment while fighting from
     the defense or during offensive operations. This table provides the reporting
     requirements for the heavy tank crew as part of the unit’s readiness.

CHAPTER 7–QUALIFICATION STANDARDS

     Chapter 7 describes how commanders determine crew qualification (or proficiency) as
     part of the unit’s readiness reporting. It provides a general overview for commanders
     making personnel manning, talent management, and crew assignment decisions that
     directly impact the qualifications of their organization.

xxiv  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025

                                                                                                         Introduction

APPENDIXES

     The appendices in this training circular provide a guide to units as they build their unit
     training plan. They provide specific information on—

          • The recommended training munitions, pyrotechnics, and simulators to conduct
                each event.

          • Alternate training methods for training in austere environments or when
                appropriate or authorized simulation systems are not available.

APPENDIX A—AMMUNITION REQUIREMENTS

     This appendix describes the recommended training ammunition, pyrotechnics,
     simulators, and other munitions that support the training strategy. Appendix A provides
     a detailed listing of munitions by weapon, system, or platform and includes various
     planning considerations.

APPENDIX B—TRACKING AND MANIPULATION BOARDS

     Appendix B describes locally manufactured or acquired tracking boards used in austere
     environments or when authorized simulation systems are not available for training. It
     describes a means of completing Table II, Situational Training Exercise–Virtual, using
     legacy training methods. It provides an example exercise that will satisfy the training
     objectives of Table II in less that optimal conditions.

APPENDIX C—DEPLOYED/AUSTERE QUALIFICATION

     This appendix provides the targetry for units authorized to conduct a qualification in
     austere environments. It describes the approval authority and circumstances when this
     course of fire is appropriate.

APPENDIX D—BASIC SKILLS ENGAGEMENTS

     Appendix D is an optional menu of basic skills engagement that may be used for
     additional training, re-training, or cross-training crews. They are not resourced with
     training ammunition. These engagements are options to the commander for use when
     time, facilities, and training munitions are available.

APPENDIX E—COMPLEX ENGAGEMENTS

     Appendix E provides an overview of mastery engagements that can be fired by a unit
     provided resources are available. These ten engagements in Table C, Complex
     Engagements, are designed by Master Gunners for use with experienced crews at the
     direction of the brigade commander or above.

APPENDIX F—FORMS

     This appendix describes the Department of the Army forms referenced within this
     manual where this publication is the proponent. It provides detailed instructions for their
     completion, routing, retention, and submission

                                      Chapter 1

              Table I—Gunnery Skills Test

    Understanding the weapons and weapon system platforms is instrumental in
    the development of the warrior skills trained in marksmanship and gunnery.
    The information taught and evaluated in the gunnery skills test (GST) for
    crew platforms teaches, tests, and evaluates the critical live-fire tasks that
    increase Soldiers’ proficiency. The GST instructs Soldiers on how to
    maintain, operate, and correct malfunctions of the platform’s weapons, as
    well as the procedures and performance measures required to place the
    vehicle in operation for live-fire event. It includes safety-specific tasks that
    reinforce the proper procedures in the event of an emergency on the vehicle.
    Chapter 1 outlines how to conduct the GST for the M1A2 Abrams platform.
    Soldiers must complete the GST between training week/month minus the
    number of weeks/months until execution (in this case, 6 weeks [T-6]) and
    gunnery execution (active component weeks and reserve component
    months) before conducting any live-fire range training including sub-caliber
    or zeroing procedures for the platform.

OVERVIEW OF TABLE I

     1-1. The crew platform, Table I, GST, is hands-on demonstration of proficiency and
     written evaluation of weaponry tasks, characteristics, capabilities, and vehicle safety
     related skills that serves as a prerequisite to any live-fire event. It verifies the Soldier’s
     ability to perform critical tasks, has the basic understanding of combat identification,
     emergency procedures, and other vital skills to participate in live-fire training as part of
     a crew.

                           LIVE-FIRE PREREQUISITE

          Table I is a prerequisite for any live-fire event, including zero
          procedures, sub-caliber or in-bore training, or any live-fire
          maintenance procedure. Units must maintain the training records
          and results, for the longer of 12 months, or through the next testing
          event.

     1-2. Training prior to Table I is a platoon and company or troop responsibility. Table I,
     GST, is a test that serves as the commander’s official record of training proficiency prior
     to entering a live-fire training density.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  1


---

Chapter 1

     1-3. Units conduct GST quarterly to maintain proficiency. Units must complete Table I,
     GST, for record between T-6 and training execution [T-execution (commencement
     day)], or the week of training execution (T-week), of the live-fire event. Units can
     include additional tasks they require to support the training program but cannot eliminate
     tasks for any weapon system. Figure 1-1 shows where Table I resides withing the heavy
     tank training strategy through crew qualification.

                          Figure 1-1. Table I, gunnery skills test

   1-4. Commanders require all Soldiers to conduct the Table I, GST tasks from all crew
   positions. This allows Soldiers to change positions when required during training. This
   training strategy helps the commander to overcome such circumstances as crew changes
   or unforeseen absences of assigned Soldiers.

   1-5. Evaluators not participating in live-fire tables must pass the GST task they are
   evaluating within the same standard as above. Evaluators who are part of a firing crew
   must pass the remaining GST tasks pertaining to their platform prior to live-fire training.
   The unit’s Master Gunner or designated noncommissioned officer in charge (NCOIC)
   must validate all evaluators.

2  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                  Table I—Gunnery Skills Test

PURPOSE OF TABLE I

     1-6. The GST is a skills assessment and risk mitigation tool for the commander. It assists
     the commander in determining the safe and effective use of the platform, its weapons,
     identification of threats and common ammunition by the crewmembers in the unit.
     Further, it identifies shortfalls and training gaps in those gunnery specific skills, which
     build crew proficiency and unit readiness.

METHOD FOR TABLE I

     1-7. Table I is a hands-on, performance-based evaluation of basic gunnery skills to the
     Army-standard of knowledge or skill. It utilizes experienced evaluators to educate, train,
     test, and retrain Soldiers to meet or exceed those standards.

END STATE FOR TABLE I

     1-8. Each Soldier assigned as a member of the crew has successfully demonstrated the
     skills and knowledge required to continue training on the platform through platform
     qualification. Commanders are confident in their Soldier’s abilities in the position
     assigned.

     1-9. The tasks evaluated in Table I provide the commander a means to certify the crew’s
     basic weapons platform proficiency prior to live-fire training. It is a guide for identifying
     individual and crew weapons platform strengths and weaknesses on specific
     fundamentals. The commander and Master Gunner when structuring the unit’s crew
     training program should use the results from the GST.

TASK, CONDITIONS, AND STANDARD FOR TABLE I

     1-10. All personnel assigned to a vehicle crew, regardless of military occupational
     specialty (MOS), who intend to participate in any live-fire event, must pass Table 1,
     GST. To pass the GST, Soldiers must receive a GO on all tasks specific to the platform
     from which they will execute live-fire training (see figure 1-2).

Figure 1-2. Table I, gunnery skills test task, conditions, and standard

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120                                             3


---

Chapter 1

PLAN FOR TABLE I

     1-11. This section details the resources needed for planning purposes. It includes general
     information on the following topics for consideration:

          • Uniform—he minimum uniform requirements for the training event.
          • Facilities—available facilities that support the training event.
          • Training plan—the minimum tasks, execution guidelines or special instructions

                for the training event.
          • Time and throughput—the estimated time required to complete the training

                event including throughput considerations for planning.
          • Ammunition—any training munitions required to complete the training event.

                This includes dummy, drilled, inert (DDI) items that require coordination. This
                ammunition may have to be requested in the total ammunition management
                information system.
          • Training aids, devices, simulators, simulations (TADSS)—any available
                training aids that directly support or augment the training event.

     1-12. Units may require coordination with installation support activities such as the
     training support center for devices or training aids, or the ammunition supply point for
     DDI items. Each installation’s policies and procedures vary. Units should reference their
     supporting installation’s training standard operating procedure (SOP) or policies for
     detailed information.

     1-13. Units may require updating their signature cards, hand receipts, or other
     documentation to ensure the required resources are on order, secured, or drawn in
     advance of the training event. Additionally, installations may require additional training
     to draw or utilize some of the TADSS. Sufficient time for those installation or unit
     specific training requirements should be included in the planning process.

     1-14. The battalion or squadron commander determines whether the GST is a diagnostic
     or a record test using the following considerations. The battalion or squadron
     commander may direct decentralized execution to the company or troop level. The
     purposes for GSTs include the following:

          • Units perform diagnostic GST events quarterly to maintain proficiency when
                not within T-6 through T-execution of a live-fire event.

          • A diagnostic GST identifies the unit’s strengths and weaknesses.
          • Diagnostic GSTs provide information for the direct-fire training program.
          • Diagnostic GSTs serve to assess newly assigned personnel.
          • A record GST allows commanders to meet table prerequisites to certify

                proficiency of Soldiers before conducting any live-fire training.

4  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025

Chapter 1

TABLE I TIME AND THROUGHPUT

     1-18. Plan GSTs to maximize training time and resources. The training unit must fully
     understand all evaluation procedures and criteria. Units are responsible for training and
     certifying the evaluators prior to conducting Table I as part of the 8-step training model.

     1-19. To properly evaluate GSTs, units must consider the following factors:
          • Administrative requirements.
          • Evaluator availability based on requirements and certifications.
          • Test locations.
          • Resource requirements.
          • Time.
          • Retraining.
          • Retests.

     1-20. Incorporate sufficient time for training, testing, retraining, and retesting into the
     plan based on the projected number of Soldiers to evaluate. The goal when taking the
     GST is to demonstrate proficiency on key tasks (individual and crew) to execute safe
     and effective live-fire events.

     1-21. During the GST process, the Master Gunner may combine tasks at a given station
     to increase throughput and maximize available training time. The tasks of clearing,
     disassembling, assembling, conducting a function check, and loading and unloading a
     weapon system is an example of combining individual tasks into a single station.
     Combining tasks provides flexibility to Master Gunners or NCOICs in the planning and
     execution of their GST.

     1-22. Crews in their assigned positions complete all collective tasks. Units may evaluate
     additional crewmembers in gunner or vehicle commander (VC) positions after their
     testing in the assigned position are complete. This provides additional flexibility to the
     commander to manage available talent within the organization and prepare for back-up
     manning possibilities.

     1-23. Figure 1-6 provides an example if only one vehicle is used for a notional test that
     requires 28 minutes per Soldier to complete. This includes the administrative and testing
     time for the task.

Chapter 1

PREPARE FOR TABLE I

     1-27. The personnel, equipment, and material listed below are required for all tasks
     are—

          • The primary evaluator, staff sergeant or above, who is responsible for
                administering the overall test.
                 This is usually the Master Gunner or designated NCOIC.
                 This evaluator may occupy a test station.

          • The station evaluator, sergeant or above (one per test station).
          • A test site as required by tasks.
          • A stopwatch (one per evaluator).
          • The criterion scoring checklist (one per Soldier).
          • TC 3-20.31-1 (one per station).
          • Dummy rounds (as required).

             Note. The test administrative guide lists additional items, if required, for each
             particular station. This can include items such as desks, chairs, clipboards,
             pencils, and applicable references.

DETERMINE RESOURCES

     1-28. The Master Gunner, or NCOIC, must determine equipment and resources needed
     to support the test site and conduct the testing to standard. The condition section of each
     individual station contains the equipment requirements. Examples of additional support
     requirements are—

          • Vehicles with drivers.
          • Transportation to and from the test site.
          • Tents for warm-up and briefings.
          • Tables for scoring.
          • Chairs.
          • Medical support.
          • Class I support.
          • TADSS.
          • TC 3-20.31-1 and applicable publications for each testing station.

     1-29. Once the needed resources have been identified, they must coordinate the use of
     personnel and equipment. This coordination usually is made through the chain of
     command and supply sergeant or the company or battalion operations staff officer, and
     the logistics staff officer.

IDENTIFY THE TEST SITE

     1-30. Establish a test site that supports the testing of all stations. Units should consider
     the number of Soldiers for evaluation to ensure the selected site supports the throughput
     required.

12  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                  Table I—Gunnery Skills Test

CONSTRUCT A TEST STATION DIAGRAM (FLOW CHART)

     1-31. Design a test station diagram or flow chart prior to any test rehearsal (see
     figure 1-9 on page 14). A diagram facilitates management and control of the test and
     aids in briefing Soldiers and evaluators. The diagram should include the following:

          • Administrative area.
          • Classrooms.
          • Test stations.
          • Retraining location.
          • Movement schedule with direction of rotation, if applicable.
          • Latrines.
          • Aid station and warm-up tent, if available.

     1-32. When developing a GST site, evaluators should pay particular attention to
     retraining vehicles and back-up vehicles.

     1-33. Position retraining vehicles in such a way for use for any task requiring retraining
     or retesting, which is important when training, or testing boresight and zero. For
     example, if the personnel in the retraining vehicle cannot see the boresight panel or the
     vehicle is not at the correct distance, the retraining capability of that vehicle will not be
     effective.

     1-34. Calculate back-up vehicles into the development of a GST site to address any
     critical failures of any primary vehicles dedicated to any task. In the event of a
     malfunction or catastrophic failure of a primary vehicle, the back-up vehicle is readily
     available to allow the GST to continue with limited loss of throughput.

     1-35. Figure 1-9 on page 14 shows an example test station diagram with the test station
     groups. In this example, it provides a general timeline for execution at the company or
     troop level, rotating platoons through the test stations. This diagram is useful to brief the
     conduct of the event, briefing noncommissioned officer books for visitors, and in the
     administrative area.

SELECT EVALUATORS

     1-36. NCOICs should select evaluators as far in advance as possible. This gives the
     NCOIC time to train, to select the most qualified personnel available, and to coordinate
     with other units, if necessary. Early selections, in turn, allow evaluators time to prepare.
     If the NCOIC must draw on resources from outside their unit, they should request
     assistance from their company command group to make this coordination to ensure
     proper channel utilization for their request. After the evaluators have been selected, the
     NCOIC briefs them on their duties and responsibilities, the test schedule and location
     (using a flow chart or test station diagram), and the station they will be testing. The
     NCOIC must provide training and testing dates to certify the proficiency of the
     evaluators.

TEST EVALUATORS

     1-37. Evaluators must be technically proficient in performing all tasks to standard.
     Typically, the GST occurs over multiple training days. Due to personnel requirements
     and availability issues, training, testing and certification of each evaluator on the specific
     GST station they are to evaluate occurs prior to the unit testing date. Each evaluator
     should test on all GST stations in case another evaluator becomes unavailable during
     testing time.

CONDUCT A REHEARSAL

     1-38. Units must rehearse the test before administering it because of the size and scope.
     Units should conduct the rehearsal at the test site with all evaluators present. The NCOIC
     should check each station to make sure the evaluator is aware of their duties and
     responsibilities, the stations are set up correctly with all required equipment, support and
     test materials are on hand, and execution of each station is according to the test
     administrative guides.

     1-39. The rehearsal day also may serve to train, test, and certify all evaluators, which
     also satisfies their live-fire GST prerequisite.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  15

Chapter 1

EXECUTE TABLE I

     1-40. Before the Soldiers arrive, evaluators set up the equipment and materials needed
     at each station. The evaluator should have a crew roster by vehicle bumper number and
     position at their station to track the Soldiers. Evaluators provide the Soldiers with all the
     necessary materials and equipment as outlined in this chapter and in the test
     administrative guide for each station.

     1-41. The evaluator must read the instructions for their assigned station exactly as
     written in the guide. The evaluator must use the criteria-scoring checklist provided for
     each task to evaluate each Soldier’s performance to standard. Once testing is complete,
     the evaluator records the results on DA Form 7758 (Gunnery Skills Test [GST]
     Individual Roll-up). As each Soldier finishes or the time limit is up, whichever occurs
     first, the evaluator checks the Soldier’s performance as either GO or NO-GO, informs
     them of their performance on that task, and directs them to the next station or to
     retraining.

     1-42. The company Master Gunner or designated NCOIC, is responsible for completing
     DA Form 7758 and maintaining platoon and company roll-up information. They ensure
     that all collected roll-ups are submitted to the next higher headquarters.

     1-43. Before testing, the NCOIC must make sure the stations are set up and all
     equipment and support requirements are available. The NCOIC also must brief the
     Soldiers on safety, rotational order, hours of testing, and location of the stations.
     Preferably, Soldiers should separate into small groups or crews and be assigned to initial
     test stations. During testing, the NCOIC will spot check each station to ensure the task
     standard and attendance rosters, with correctly annotated scorecards, are being
     maintained.

ASSESS—TABLE I

     1-44. After action reviews (AARs), are the key to success in any training event, as well
     as improving the event for future iterations. There are two primary AARs for
     Table I: testing Soldier or crew AAR and the Table I execution AAR. Evaluators must
     conduct an AAR after the completion of each task for each Soldier or crew, and for the
     NCOIC of Table I upon completion of unit training. Without these immediate AARs,
     valuable lessons for the Soldier, crew, and unit can be lost.

SOLDIER INDIVIDUAL TASK AARS

     1-45. At the completion of each task, the evaluator will provide feedback to each Soldier
     on their performance at the station. Whether a Soldier passes or fails to complete a GST
     task, the evaluator should be prepared to offer guidance on how the Soldier can improve
     their skills.

16  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                  Table I—Gunnery Skills Test

TABLE I–EVENT AFTER ACTION REVIEW

     1-46. The battalion or brigade Master Gunner, designated NCOIC, or operations
     sergeant major should conduct an AAR to help the NCOIC understand their actions and
     interactions during the conduct of the GST. The AAR should be conducted prior to any
     debrief to the unit commander and should consist of a discussion of the strengths and
     weaknesses of the training event. This allows the NCOIC to debrief the commander with
     accurate training results and recommendations to improve the testing process for future
     events.

     1-47. The results of the GST provide the commander with additional quantifiable testing
     information to assess unit proficiency. Combined and integrate the GST results and
     AAR comments to improve future training, testing, or evaluation.

PREPARE THE DEBRIEF

     1-48. Once the test is complete, the NCOIC prepares a debriefing on the GST results for
     the commander. Units retain this information for training records and maintain the
     information throughout the weapons training density. The NCOIC collects the Soldier’s
     scorecards as soon as they complete testing and prior to anyone leaving the test site. The
     evaluators can provide additional information about a Soldier’s test performance. After
     all data is collected, the NCOIC prepares a summary of results, interpreting information
     from test results to determine the strengths and weaknesses of the unit. This report will,
     at a minimum, contain the following data:

          • A roster by duty position of each Soldier tested.
          • The scores of each Soldier tested.
          • The percentage of Soldiers passing the GST.
          • Percentage of GOs per station.
          • Percentage of NO-GOs per station.

RECORD INDIVIDUAL TASK PERFORMANCE—TABLE I

     1-50. Leaders track and train their Soldier’s individual and collective tasks trained and
     evaluated using tools provided by the Army Training Network (ATN) such as the small
     unit leader tool (SULT) and the digital job book (DJB). These systems interface directly
     with the Digital Training Management System (DTMS) to record training and
     evaluation results.

             Note. Leaders must be granted access by their commander or DTMS manager
             to enroll or assign their Soldiers the specific tasks from the individual critical
             task list (ICTL) that are trained and tested during the training event.

     1-51. From within the SULT, the authorized leader is enabled to view, maintain, and
     update:

          • Warrior tasks and battle drills.
          • MOS-specific individual critical tasks.
          • Unit-mandated training tasks.
          • Supporting training and evaluation outlines (T&EOs) (access dependent on

                proponent distribution restrictions).

     1-52. Leaders should update their Soldier’s individual training records for training and
     evaluation of the following tasks, based on their assigned position. This is not an
     all-inclusive list of the trained and tested tasks for this event. Leaders should adjust
     based on the unit’s additions to the event.

     1-53. This ensures historical data is available throughout the Army force generation
     training cycle for the chain of command to assess strengths and weaknesses of crews
     and address these concerns in upcoming GST training. The most current GST records
     must accompany the unit or crew throughout the entire weapons training density.

                                      Chapter 2

 Table II—Situational Training Exercise–Virtual

    Crew platform, Table II, Situational Training Exercise-Virtual (STX-V) is
    a simulations-based demonstration of performance of employing the
    element to its primary capabilities. Table II itself is the culminating
    evaluation of the crew after completing the appropriate simulations training
    strategy within the simulation system itself. Table II is a replication
    (simulation) of the crew’s live-fire qualification course. It provides a
    measured demonstration of performance prior to any live-fire event for the
    element.

OVERVIEW OF TABLE II

     2-1. The purpose of crew platform, Table II, STX-V, is to ensure the crew possesses the
     skills and experience necessary to execute live-fire events and exercises safely, which
     implies a functional understanding of the engagement process, fire commands,
     engagement techniques, and lethality. This is the culminating test of their abilities and
     skills on their platform in simulation after completing the appropriate simulations
     training plan.

                           LIVE-FIRE PREREQUISITE

          Table II is a prerequisite for any live-fire event, including zero
          procedures, sub-caliber or in-bore training, or any live-fire
          maintenance procedure. Units must maintain the training records
          and results, for the longer of 12 months, or through the next testing
          event.

     2-2. Table II is completed after successful advancement in the approved simulation
     system’s training model. Table II is the GLTF exercise within that training system which
     simulates the qualification course of fire. How Table II is tied to the other training events
     within the crew training strategy is shown in figure 2-1 on page 26.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  25


---

Chapter 2

                                     Figure 2-1. Table II, STX-V

PURPOSE OF TABLE II

     2-3. Table II, STX-V, is a skills assessment and risk mitigation tool for the commander.
     It assists the commander in determining the safe and effective use of the platform, its
     weapons, identification of threats and common ammunition by the crewmembers in the
     unit. Further, it identifies shortfalls and training gaps in those gunnery specific skills,
     which build crew proficiency and unit readiness.

METHOD FOR TABLE II

     2-4. Table II is a hands-on, performance-based evaluation of basic gunnery skills to the
     Army-standard of knowledge or skill in a simulator system (crew shelter). It utilizes
     experienced evaluators to educate, train, test, and retrain Soldiers to meet or exceed
     those standards.

END STATE FOR TABLE II

     2-5. The VC and gunner assigned as a crew have successfully demonstrated the skills
     and knowledge required to continue training on the platform through platform
     qualification. Commanders are confident in their Soldier’s abilities in the position
     assigned.

     2-6. The tasks evaluated in Table II provide the commander a means to certify the crew’s
     basic weapons platform proficiency prior to live-fire training. It is used as a guide for
     identifying individual and crew weapons platform strengths and weaknesses on specific
     engagement techniques and employment of the vehicle’s fire control system capabilities
     and enablers.

26         TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025

PLAN FOR TABLE II

     2-8. This section details the resources needed for planning purposes. Planning requires
     consideration of the following topics:

          • Uniform—the minimum uniform requirements for the training event.
          • Facilities—available facilities that support the training event.
          • Crew Experience—units must consider their crew’s experience (VC and

                gunner) when creating the plan for Table II. The experience of the crew directly
                affects the throughput of the event, and drives the engagement scenarios
                development, when required.
          • Training plan—the minimum tasks, sequence, and method of execution for the
                training event.
          • Time and throughput—the estimated time required to complete the training
                event including throughput considerations for planning.
          • Ammunition—any training munitions required to complete the training event.
                This includes DDI items that require coordination.
          • TADSS—any available training aids that directly support or augment the
                training event.

UNIFORM FOR TABLE II

     2-9. Crews must be in the appropriate vehicle crewmember uniform or prescribed unit
     uniform and protective mask. Crews are not required to have any additional body armor
     or gear inside the crew shelter or on their vehicle when using the T&MBs for testing.
     Figure 2-3 provides the minimum uniform training requirements for all simulations
     training, including the GTLF.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120                               29

Chapter 2

FACILITIES FOR TABLE II

     2-10. Table II is conducted in an approved primary simulation system. Unit training in
     austere environment without access to a primary device may conduct training with an
     approved alternate device. Figure 2-4 shows the primary and alternate facilities or
     methods to complete STX-V.

           Figure 2-4. Table Il primary and alternate simulations systems

     2-11. Units may use the embedded training system, if equipped, for Table II if not
     equipped with the advanced gunnery training system. Units provided the advanced
     gunnery training system may not use the embedded trainer system to meet their Table II
     requirements; however, they are encouraged to use embedded trainer systems in
     conjunction with the advanced gunnery training system for sustainment and cross
     training of their crews.

Simulations Requirements

     2-12. If conducting Table II, GTLF virtually, the use of a precision gunnery trainer is
     required. This ensures that platform systems, weapons functionality, and ballistics are

30  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                         Table II—Situational Training Exercise–Virtual

     accurately represented to the crew to prevent crews from developing any poor habits
     that will affect lethality on their platform.

Precision Gunnery Trainer

     2-13. A precision gunnery trainer is a system, stand-alone or embedded, comprised of
     virtual environment and physical components which enables the crew to engage targets
     using all modes of their vehicle’s fire control system. Ballistic solutions and effects,
     kinematic lead, stabilization, target handoff, target designation, sight capabilities, and
     limitations are all accurately represented in the system. The system must accurately
     represent the form, fit, and function of the crew area within the vehicle. It incorporates
     an AAR capability which allows the evaluator and crewmembers to see the placement
     of each round in relation to the target, its corresponding data, and their engagement score
     in accordance with (IAW) applicable platform manual. While the primary purpose of
     the system is to train the crew on the direct fire engagement process, the real feel of
     switches, levers, hand stations, sights, and the room available to maneuver inside the
     vehicle is a critical component of basic crew proficiency.

Collective Virtual Trainer

     2-14. A virtual collective trainer is a system, comprised of multiple platforms or vehicles
     replicated within the virtual environment. The system should have the ability to replicate
     air, ground, and dismounted systems and soldiers. It is comprised of both physical and
     virtual components which accurately represent the equipment Soldiers and
     crewmembers operate with. The system must enable collective units to train “shoot-
     move-communicate” in a virtual battlespace that accurate represents their operating
     environment where the commander can evaluate their ability to complete tactical tasks
     to standard. It incorporates a recording and AAR capability to enable commanders to
     review, analyze, and evaluate the performance of their units. It must give commanders
     the ability to incorporate branches, drivers, and sequels into their mission execution. The
     system also must contain the ability for the leader to command and control their training
     units within the virtual environment.

     2-15. There are several different types of virtual trainers. Figure 2-5 on page 32 lists the
     capabilities for precision gunnery and collective virtual trainers.

Chapter 2

CREW EXPERIENCE

     2-17. Units that develop scenarios on games for training systems must consider the level
     of experience of their training crews. Crews are evaluated on their experience by
     position and are identified in paragraphs 2-18 through 2-20.

New Crew

     2-18. A crew where the VC and gunner is new to their position on the vehicle. New
     crews begin training in the simulations and gaming systems at the entry level to build
     crew coordination, engagement skills, and cohesion.

Turbulent Crew

     2-19. A crew where both the VC and gunner have previous experience and have
     qualified within the last 12 months in those positions but have not worked together as a
     crew. Assessment of these crews for proper placement within the simulation’s training
     management system is required. Typically, the assessment shows they should begin
     training at the lowest common training scenario both the gunner and VC have
     successfully completed to maximize their training time. For crews using gaming
     systems, the unit must develop and execute scenarios that are more difficult for the crew.
     This ensures the crews remain challenged in the simulations and gaming system and
     continue to learn direct fire engagement techniques and procedures.

Experienced Crew

     2-20. The VC and gunner previously qualified within the past 12 months. Crews using
     simulations must continue training through the next GTLF prior to executing any
     live-fire event. For crews using gaming systems, the unit must develop and execute
     scenarios that are more challenging for the crew.

             Note. Crews that have previously qualified cannot restart the simulations
             training program. They must continue training within the simulation system
             to the next higher GTLF.

     2-21. To increase crew experience during the execution of the training strategy, units
     use certified VCEs. Properly trained and certified VCEs are an asset to the commander.
     Performing duties as a VCE in any capacity builds the experience of the VCE as well.
     It exposes the VCE to dozens of engagements to review in depth during the evaluation
     process that would normally require the VCE to fire.

34  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025

                                                         Table II—Situational Training Exercise–Virtual

TRAINING PLAN FOR TABLE II

     2-22. Table II, STX-V, is a simulations-based exercise that replicates the engagements
     of the Table VI, Qualification. Within a crew shelter simulator system is a specific
     scenario set that replicates the training requirements and engagement skills of the
     qualification course of fire.

     2-23. Successful completion of prescribed exercise or series of exercises in the simulator
     is mandatory as a prerequisite to firing live ammunition. These exercises evaluate the
     crew’s proficiency employing its weapon system or platform against common threats,
     provide engagements under chemical, biological, radiological, and nuclear conditions,
     enhance and evaluate crew coordination, evaluate crew drills, and hone the crew’s
     engagement techniques.

System Generated Gate to Live-Fire Scenarios

     2-24. The primary simulation system module automatically generates the scenario for
     Table II. The system provides the scenarios after successful completion of the
     simulations training strategy through the minimum training requirements for live fire.
     This is accomplished through the system’s matrix movement rules, reticle aim
     requirements, and system management ratings by crew.

     2-25. For units utilizing the games for training system, the simulations center will
     develop and build the scenario for the GTLF event. The unit should proof the scenario,
     provide appropriate administrative prompts and tactical scripts (platoon fire commands)
     for each engagement, and other instructions for the crews and their evaluators to follow.
     These should be similar to the live-fire Table VI event and will serve as a verification
     of those scripts prior live-fire training.

     2-26. Figure 2-7 on page 36 shows the minimum engagements for system generated
     simulations or the baseline standard engagements for unit created scenarios. A guideline
     for creating day and night phase scenarios using these engagements can be found in the
     crew platform training publication, TC 3-20.31.

Unit Developed Gate to Live-Fire Scenarios

     2-27. Each of the scenarios developed must follow the engagement and scenario
     guidelines found in this manual and in TC 3-20.31. Each scenario provides five
     engagements for day (unlimited visibility) and five engagements for night (limited
     visibility). Units must focus on manipulation of the weapon’s thermal optic and allow
     crews to use the thermal sensors during the day engagements. If the system can provide
     degraded modes of operation, a minimum of three engagements should focus on those
     skills.

     2-28. Figure 2-8 shows the environmental conditions, platform capabilities, and threat
     conditions that are replicated within simulation systems. Units building their own
     training plans within the simulation system must exercise each of the components listed
     to create a comprehensive training plan for their crews.

 Figure 2-8. Simulation system conditions and capabilities replicated

2-29. When the simulation system does not provide a generated scenario for the GTLF,
the unit Master Gunner is required to create the scenario based on the unit’s upcoming
Table VI, qualification, scenario as the baseline (start point). Units should revise the
engagements based on past performance and the experience level of the VC and gunner
combinations within the formation.

Simulation System Instructor/Operators

     2-31. Units are required to use certified instructor/operators on the simulation systems
     they plan on using. Those instructor/operators are required to receive the system training
     on their system. They must be certified VCEs as outlined in TC 3-20.31.

               Instructor/Operators must be VCE Certified

          A certified VCE or Master Gunner evaluates all simulations and
          games for training GTLF exercises. This reinforces the VCE training
          program, provides certified VCEs with additional practical
          application and experience, and ensures the crews receive
          accurate AARs comparable to those they should receive on the
          firing range.

     2-32. Crews must successfully pass all scenarios according to the standards in
     TC 3-20.31. The unit’s VCEs evaluate all crews IAW:

          • TC 3-20.31.
          • TC 3-20.31-043.

Crew Records

     2-33. Units must maintain the training records for Table II, including the simulation
     system unit back-up and the DA Form 8265 (Common Crew Score Sheet) and
     DA Form 8265-1 (Common Crew Roll-Up) for each crew completing their GTLF for 12
     months. Some primary devices only provide a unit roll-up with summary of each crew’s
     performance.

     2-34. Where the simulation system does not generate the DA Form 8265 and
     DA Form 8265-1 forms as an automatic output, the instructor evaluators are responsible
     for completing those forms during the GTLF scenarios.

TIME AND THROUGHPUT FOR TABLE II

     2-35. Simulations have specific authorized hours per month, shown in figure 2-10.
     These are the standard simulations-use requirements for all crew platforms.

                Figure 2-10. Simulations use allocations by component
             Note. DA PAM 350-38 specifies simulator training requirements and skills
             sustainment intervals for weapons and weapon system platforms.
     2-36. When completing Table II’s GTLF exercise, units should expect each crew
     requiring 2 hours to complete the training with AAR. This assumes the crew arrives to
     the training facility, receives the training and testing instructions, conducts any
     alignment procedures required of the simulation system, and a series of warm-up
     exercises prior to testing. This also includes the AAR at the completion of the training
     scenario by the instructor/operator, or the VCE. It does not include any retraining or
     retesting time.

TADSS FOR TABLE II

     2-37. Crewmembers conduct training prior to and for Table II using the approved
     primary simulator system. In extreme circumstances with approval from the brigade
     commander or above, they may utilize an approved alternate system or device.

     2-38. Use of these systems are to train the VC and gunner on individual and
     crew-coordination skills, engagement techniques, and target acquisition. When using
     games for training systems, the unit should request comparable gunner control handles
     to support additional form, fit, and function to the training and evaluation.

     2-39. Units that do not have access to a primary or alternate simulation systems or are
     training in an austere environment, should complete training using a T&MB.

     2-40. The design, layout, and function of the T&MB are listed in appendix B. It includes
     a sample scenario for unit use or modification. Original files for the information on the
     T&MB that can be modified by the unit can be downloaded from the weapons and
     gunnery teams page (see the references at the back of this manual).

     2-41. Unit must create a memorandum for record stating the crew’s completion of Table
     II with the unit created training plan using the T&MB. This memorandum approved by
     the battalion or squadron commander and is included in each crew’s gunnery packet (see
     TC 3-20.31).

                        Austere Environment Training

          Use T&MB when no simulation system is available or accessible to
          the unit or the platform type. Use T&MBs at the range for concurrent
          training or in austere environments to support the unit training plan.

     2-42. Figure 2-11 on page 42 lists the various versions of the tracking and manipulation
     board versions. It shows an example of one version as well as a sample layout for use.

PREPARE FOR TABLE II

     2-43. Units execute the Table II, STX-V in one of two ways: with an authorized (primary
     or alternate) simulation system or T&MBs. The primary trainer is the company
     commander with and assigned VCEs. Units evaluate the Table II event internally and
     require the company to coordinate for training of their VCEs.

     2-44. The platoon leadership in coordination with the unit Master Gunner are
     responsible for training their crews on the conduct of fire, engagement techniques, and
     movement techniques.

     2-45. Units provide the following to prepare the trainers:
          • Commander training objectives.
          • Evaluation firing order or window for completing Table II.
          • Tasks for training and evaluation (when using T&MBs).
          • Risk assessment.
          • Identification of supporting doctrinal publications for use as primary
                references.
          • T&MBs, when required.

TRAIN THE TRAINER—TABLE II

     2-46. Table II relies on gunner and VCs to be dual certified as instructor/operators of
     available simulator systems and VCEs.

     2-47. Units schedule VCE training for their gunners and VCs. Each battalion will
     establish policies and procedures to train, certify, and recertify their VCEs to ensure
     common instruction from the Army-standard vehicle crew evaluation exportable
     package (see TC 3-20.31).

     2-48. Unit Master Gunners maintain a roster of certified VCEs for the unit. They manage
     the recertification, as appropriate, based on unit policies and processes. Master Gunners
     should cross train drivers on VCE tasks to support range operations on maneuver ranges.

     2-49. Where simulator systems are available, the Master Gunner schedules
     instructor/operator courses for those systems. Units should maintain instructor/operator
     and VCE certifications current for all assigned gunners and VCs, including platoon
     leaders, the executive officer, and commander.

     2-50. Information on all the train the trainer and leader certifications required are listed
     in detail in TC 3-20.31. Units should review those training and certification requirements
     when preparing for training.

RECONNAISSANCE OF THE SITE—TABLE II

     2-51. The Table II, STX-V uses an authorized simulation system. Units allow sufficient
     time scheduled to ensure all crews train, test, and retest as necessary within T-6 to
     T-execution training window. The crew shelter simulations systems, typically a
     battalion or brigade asset, will require coordination with battalion or brigade for use.
     Commanders schedule the training location as far in advance as practical following the
     installation’s policies and procedures.

     2-52. Once scheduled, the commander, Master Gunner, and platoon leaders conduct a
     reconnaissance of the training site to—

          • Verify sufficient space is available to support training.
          • Accomplish multiple iterations or rotations through the scenario with

                appropriate retraining opportunities.
          • Ensure sufficient administrative areas are available to support group

                discussions, briefings, and AARs.
          • Determine the tracking method of completed crews.
          • Identify the process for completing the appropriate score cards, crew roll-up,

                and platoon training records as required.

                     Tracking and Manipulation Boards

          When an approved simulation system is not available or accessible,
          the unit will utilize the T&MBs. This will require a 25-meter by
          25-meter area to set up the panels for the 160-degree frontal arc
          and allow for multiple vehicle use. See appendix B for detailed
          information on the T&MBs including their design, placement, layout,
          use, and a sample training scenario.

EXECUTE TABLE II

     2-53. Before the Soldiers arrive, VCEs set up the equipment and materials needed for
     the evaluation of the GTLF. The evaluator should have a crew roster by vehicle bumper
     number and position at their station to track crews and provide daily status updates to
     the company.

SIMULATOR GATE TO LIVE-FIRE SCENARIO

     2-54. The evaluator must read the instructions exactly as written in the guide within the
     system instructions or provided by the unit. Evaluators are not authorized to assist in
     any of the following crew functions during the evaluation, such as—

          • Target acquisition.
          • Target detection.
          • Subsequent target locations or intelligence.
          • Driver movement techniques.
          • Sensing of the rounds or machine gun bursts.

     2-55. The evaluator must use the system generated or hard copy DA Form 8265 and
     DA Form 8265-1 to record each engagement and the overall scenario. Records are to be
     maintained in the crew’s gunnery folder for 12 months. Units may also include the unit
     training roll-up as part of the training historical records.

TRACKING AND MANIPULATION BOARDS—TABLE II

     2-56. Evaluators utilize the unit-provided criteria scoring checklist for the 10 steps of
     the tracking and manipulation board exercise. The evaluators will use specified prompts
     and evaluators may not assist the crew to perform any function.

     2-57. Once testing is complete, the evaluator records the results on a locally created
     roll-up form. As each Soldier finishes or the time limit is up, whichever occurs first, the
     evaluator checks the Soldier’s performance as either GO or NO-GO, informs them of
     their performance on that task, and directs them to the next station or to retraining.

     2-58. The company Master Gunner or designated NCOIC, is responsible for completing
     unit and maintaining platoon and company roll-up information ensuring the next higher
     headquarters is informed, if required.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  45


---

Chapter 2

ASSESS—TABLE II

     2-59. At the completion of the Table II, the evaluator will provide an AAR. The
     evaluator follows the AAR guidelines to gunnery evaluation found in TC 3-20.31 and
     includes any insights or recommendations to improve crew performance.

SIMULATOR SYSTEM EVALUATION

     2-60. When debriefing a crew in a simulation system, the evaluator must use the
     DA Form 8265 for each engagement. Evaluators may require 10 to 15 minutes to
     complete the score sheets prior to the AAR. Units may choose to rotate evaluators to
     maximize the time available in the simulator. Evaluators should include information
     on—

          • Targets, posture, and range for each engagement.
          • Target classification; most dangerous, dangerous, and least dangerous.
          • Time to acquire each target.
          • Time to decide (issue the command of execution).
          • Appropriate engagement technique.
          • Reinforcement of gunner rules of lay, aim center of visible mass, end in

                elevation, remember sight picture.
          • Opening time on primary armament and machine gun targets.
          • Closing time on machine gun targets.
          • Subsequent rounds and direct fire adjustment techniques.
          • Supplemental engagement commands and engagement techniques.
          • Ammunition selection and switchology.
          • Engagement techniques.
          • Overall performance and areas of improvement.

TRACKING AND MANIPULATION BOARD EVALUATION

     2-61. Evaluators of crews using the T&MBs should at a minimum include information
     on—

          • Tasks for each step.
          • Skills trained during each step.
          • Time to complete the tracking.
          • Penalty time for the tracking (when the reticle aim point leaves the tracking

                path).
          • Switchology errors (when the gunner removed their head from the optic brow

                pad or incorrect positioning).
          • Tracking rate or slew rate inconsistencies.
          • Reinforcement of the rules of lay.
          • Overall performance and areas of improvement.

CREW GUNNERY FOLDER RECORDS—TABLE II

     2-62. Annotation of all notes from the above training is on the score sheets provided.
     VCs are required to confirm the information by signing DA Form 8265-1 or the locally
     produced score sheet when using the T&MBs.

                                      Chapter 3

      Table III—Situational Training Exercise

    Table III, STX, is a live environment, hands on training event using training
    aids and devices, and includes the use of blank ammunition, pyrotechnics,
    and battle effects simulators (BESs). STX is a station-based event that
    consists of three distinct training and evaluation stations that contain various
    crew skills that progressively build crew proficiency. All events require both
    day and night execution. Table III, STX is designed to be conducted
    independently of the direct fire engagement event. Table III is conducted in
    a live environment in a training area with established training lanes (course
    of fire and protection tasks). It is the first training event where the crew is
    tactically operating their platform as a crew under varying conditions.

OVERVIEW OF TABLE III

     3-1. Table III verifies the crew’s ability to complete effectively basic crew engagements
     using various platform capabilities, provides for day and night driver’s training as part
     of the unit driver’s training program, and ensures the crew demonstrates proficiency
     performing specific emergency procedures on their platform. It provides time for
     training and evaluation of these skills that are the foundation to all maneuver training
     focused on the ability to move, shoot, and communicate.

     3-2. Units must complete Table III, STX, for record between T-6 and T-execution
     (T-week) of the live-fire event. Units can include additional tasks they require to support
     the training program but cannot eliminate tasks for any weapon system.

                              Live-Fire Prerequisite

          Table III is a prerequisite for any live-fire event, including zero
          procedures, sub-caliber or in-bore training, or any live-fire
          maintenance procedure. Units must maintain the training records
          and results, for the longer of 12 months, or through the next testing
          event.

     3-3. Figure 3-1 on page 52 shows where Table III resides withing the heavy tank training
     strategy through crew qualification. This is the first event with the crew operating the
     platform through a series of specific tasks that build experience with the platform, crew
     cohesion, and confidence in their platform and training. It includes a series of safety
     related tasks related to vehicle safety under various conditions.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  51


---

Chapter 3

                     Figure 3-1. Table III, situational training exercise

PURPOSE OF TABLE III

     3-4. The STX event ensures the crew demonstrates critical crew task proficiency in a
     live environment. The crew executes basic fire and maneuver, integrates indirect and
     direct fires, conducts critical tasks, and completes various emergency vehicle tasks prior
     to conducting the more advanced tasks under live-fire conditions.

METHOD FOR TABLE III

     3-5. Crew STXs use training areas with multiple training lanes or phases that train crew
     collective movement and engagement techniques in real-time and at full-tempo to build
     proficiency. Crew STXs use structured scenarios to replicate the basic engagement
     techniques using the capabilities of their platform. Crews also demonstrate proficiency
     at emergency procedures on their vehicle to build proficiency and confidence in their
     ability to react rapidly to various life-threatening situations while maneuvering their
     platform.

END STATE OF TABLE III

     3-6. Crews demonstrate in a live environment their ability to execute basic and degraded
     engagement tasks and crew drills in support of decisive action operations. The
     commander sufficiently validated the unit’s vehicle load plan and portions of the SOPs
     for casualty evacuation. Crews received sufficient day and night administrative and
     tactical movement that supports the unit driver’s training program and use of primary
     and alternate battle positions, reverse slope defense positions, and other skills.

52         TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                   Table III—Situational Training Exercise

     3-7. The commander is confident in the abilities of the subordinate leaders and elements
     to execute those trained tasks to standard on demand based on the tactical situation. The
     commander gains such confidence once live-fire crew training to build experienced,
     proficient, and lethal crews is complete.

TASK, CONDITIONS, AND STANDARD FOR TABLE III

     3-8. Figure 3-2 shows the task, conditions, and standard for Table III, STX. Units may

     previous training gaps, or to increase the training rigor.

PLAN FOR TABLE III

     3-9. Table III, STX, is a live environment, hands on training event using TADSS, and
     includes the use of blank ammunition, pyrotechnics, and BESs. STX is a station-based
     event that consists of a firing platoon, critical tasks platoon, and training support platoon.

     3-10. This section details the resources needed for planning purposes. It includes general
     information on the following topics for consideration. The resources needed are the—

          • Uniform—the minimum uniform requirements for the training event.
          • Facilities—available facilities that support the training event.
          • Training plan—the minimum tasks, general guidelines, and special instructions

                for the training event.
          • Time and throughput—the estimated time required to complete the training

                event including throughput considerations for planning.
          • Ammunition—any training munitions required to complete the training event.

                This includes DDI items that require coordination.
          • TADSS—any available training aids that directly support or augment the

                training event.

     3-11. Table III is a mandatory prerequisite for any live-fire event, including zero
     procedures, sub-caliber or in-bore training event, or any live-fire maintenance procedure
     within the crew platform strategy. Units will complete Table III between T-6 and
     T-week to support the training events.

     3-12. Units may require coordination with installation support activities such as the
     training support center for devices or training aids, or the ammunition supply point for
     DDI items. Each installation’s policies and procedures vary. Units should reference their
     supporting installation’s training SOPs or policies for detailed information.
