Chapter 3

Firing Group

     3-16. Performance of the direct fire engagements portion of the STX (firing group) is
     on the crew’s vehicle. Table design is set up to train and evaluate the crew’s ability to
     engage stationary or moving targets placed in a tactical array, during day and limited
     visibility conditions. Crews execute these tasks from the offense and defense.

     3-17. The engagements may be full-scale or scaled, depending on the usable area within
     the training area. Figure 3-6 on page 61 lists the requirements for the ten specific
     engagements. Using 3/4 scale targets, the crew’s target acquisition skills and
     engagement techniques are challenged. See TC 25-8, for additional information on
     target dimensions for scaled ranges.

     3-18. All engagements are evaluated using certified VCEs using DA Form 8265 and
     DA Form 8265-1 as the official record of training. Master Gunners must ensure the
     training records are included in each crew’s Gunnery Packet.

     3-19. The engagements listings are by capability type using the standard engagement
     naming structure listed in TC 3-20.31. It provides the requirements for each engagement,
     which include the following addressed in paragraphs 3-20 through 3-29.

Task Description—Table III

     3-20. The task description includes both the engagement number (the table number plus
     engagement task) followed by the system capability trained.

Firing Vehicle Posture—Table III

     3-21. The firing vehicle posture establishes the offense or defense condition for the
     engagement.

Firer—Table III

     3-22. The firer column identifies which crewmember is to engage the target listed.
     Targets defeated by a different crewmember listed as the firer result in an auto-zero
     penalty, failing to adhere to course conditions.

Weapon (or Ammunition)—Table III

     3-23. Identifies the weapon (coaxial, caliber .50, or primary armament ammunition type)
     that is used to defeat the threat. Design of each engagement is for a specific weapon or
     ammunition type (primary armament) for use, which relates to the crew conduct of fire,
     optic selection, engagement techniques, or other unique implied tasks. Crews that fail to
     announce the appropriate weapon or ammunition, or appropriate selection for
     subsequent or supplemental engagements may be penalized IAW TC 3-20.31.

Target Type—Table III

     3-24. The target type is the target required for the presentation, IAW TC 25-8.

58  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

              Table III—Situational Training Exercise

Target Posture—Table III

     3-25. The target posture establishes if the target is stationary, moving, or evasive for the
     engagement. See TC 3-20.31 for more information on target postures.

Range (to Target)—Table III

     3-26. The range to target is the required range (full scale or equivalent scaled range)
     from the firing vehicle to the target. For offensive engagements, this distance calculation
     is from the center of the movement box to the target. The range to target distance is
     provided in meters and is displayed as a whole number, such as 600.

     3-27. The target may be placed at the distance indicated, plus or minus 200 meters. For
     example, when the range to target is listed as 600 meters, the target may be placed
     between 400 meters and 800 meters. This allows flexibility designing the scenario on
     varying maneuver range facilities with different capabilities, target counts, emplacement
     locations, and terrain restrictions.

     3-28. No change to the engagement modifier (EM) authorized. The range bands listed
     with their applied tolerances do not require any adjustment to the EM. Units are not
     authorized to exceed the target placement tolerances.

Engagement Modifier—Table III

     3-29. The EM is the time in seconds required to achieve 100 points against the listed
     threat. To defeat the threat successfully, the crew must achieve the kill standard against
     the target and have an overall engagement time of no more than the EM plus 20 seconds.
     See TC 3-20.31 for the evaluation, timing, and scoring procedures.

Scenario Requirements for Table III

     3-30. Units may not deviate from any target type, target posture, range, and weapon
     system/ammunition type for Table III. Engagements firing may be in any sequence that
     best supports their training area, flow of the range, and time available.

     3-31. The unit options and requirements are the following:
          • Unit determines how many engagements are conducted during the day phase
                and night phase.
          • Unit determines the sequence of the engagements.
          • Unit determines which tasks are conducted during the day and night phase.
          • Unit determines which engagements will include chemical, biological,
                radiological, and nuclear conditions, and must select a minimum of one day
                and one night engagement conducted in a chemical environment.
          • BES are required for use as described in TC 3-20.31 for all vehicle target panel
                presentations.
          • Target panels are 3/4 scale at full range distances or scaled accordingly if using
                a scaled training range or training area.
          • Unit may use D-type or F-type troop targets in lieu of E-type silhouettes to
                increase training for target detection, acquisition, and reticle aim.
          • Units are required to use available force-on-target training devices or TADSS,
                including laser-based systems, laser-target interface devices.

          • All safety tasks must be conducted day and night.
          • All call for tasks must be conducted day and night.

TIME AND THROUGHPUT FOR TABLE III

     3-40. The throughput expectation of this table is 14 crews per day for 2 consecutive
     days. This allows for the appropriate amount of training, retraining, and cross training
     to provide the commander the fullest flexibility for crew management options and talent
     management opportunities prior to live fire. Depending on maintenance or other
     unforeseen issues, incorporating an additional half training day to the schedule is
     recommended, although this not resourced as part of the combined arms training
     strategy. Figure 3-10 shows the expected throughput schedule based on the number of
     vehicles for firing and the unit’s day and night engagement breakdown for the
     engagements.

         Figure 3-10. Table III throughput expectations

Note. Time requirements overall are based on a single firing lane and include
4 hours of target maintenance downtime (two blocks of 2 hours each). These
calculations are for planning purposes only. Units that are more proficient in
tower operations may have an increased throughput. Conduct the critical task
and call-for requirements (not shown) simultaneously with the more time-
consuming engagement skills tasks.

TADSS FOR TABLE III

     3-42. This course requires the use of laser based TADSS for all crews. Units with
     TADSS available must incorporate them and may not deviate from the TADSS
     requirement unless approved by the brigade commander in writing. Units should focus
     on training and reinforcing the skills in the direct fire kill chain on this table specifically
     with their thermal optics, day and night, to best prepare them for the follow-on training
     events. Figure 3-12 shows the TADSS required, recommended, and optional for

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  67

Chapter 3

Instrumentable–Multiple Integrated Laser Engagement System CVTESS

     3-43. The Instrumentable–Multiple Integrated Laser Engagement System Combat
     Vehicle Tactical Engagement Simulation System (I-MILES CVTESS) is a laser-based
     training device to for use on Abrams, Bradley, heavy tank, and opposing forces tanks
     and fighting vehicles to provide real time casualty effects. The I-MILES CVTESS is an
     evolutionary approach for replacing older systems with equipment that provides better
     training fidelity. It will reinforce crew duties, reward proper engagement techniques,
     and develop tactical maneuver skills of Armor and mechanized Infantry combined arms
     teams up to brigade level. It provides unit commanders an integrated training system in
     force-on-force and force-on-target training events at home station training areas through
     instrumented training. The system interfaces with instrumentation systems at combat
     training centers.

Laser Target Interface Device

     3-44. For use with I-MILES CVTESS or other laser-based training systems when the
     unit is conducting force-on-target training, the laser target interface device provides the
     appropriate hit recording at the target emplacement and when stimulated, signals the
     target lifting mechanism to record a hit. This is a required device on Table III.

Home Station Instrumentation Training System

     3-45. Units are encouraged to integrate the home station/Army mobile instrumentation
     training system (HITS). This system will allow for the highest fidelity AARs for the
     crew’s engagements. The HITS provides exercise management, monitoring, and control.
     It includes the ability to provide training performance feedback through onsite AARs.

     3-46. HITS is a transportable system able to enhance live training at home stations up
     to the battalion level. HITS provides instrumentation through automated tools for unit
     leaders and designated observer-controller/trainers to give performance feedback to
     training units. AARs and take-home packages can provide training performance
     feedback. As part of voice communications, HITS accepts single-channel ground and
     airborne radio systems radio transmissions.

Crew Module Unit/Crew Module Unit Recorder

     3-47. The crew module unit (CMU)/integrated player unit (IPU), and crew module unit
     recorder (CMUR) are an advanced eight channel audio and video recording device for
     combat vehicles. Some installations may refer to the CMU as the IPU for all crews. The
     CMU/IPU/CMUR can simultaneously record up to eight channels of video, one channel
     of audio, and has the capability to monitor dual 1553 data busses. The CMU/IPU/CMUR
     is equipped with a Global Positioning System that captures the vehicle’s position
     location information. The CMU/IPU is for use on a digital range complex whereas the
     CMUR does not require any external support. The CMUR has an integrated AAR suite
     and stores information on a solid-state drive. The AAR suite uploads the data from the
     solid-state drive allowing a full AAR presentation.

68  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025

PREPARE FOR TABLE III

     3-48. Units execute the crew STX in a variety of ways. Typically, each platoon conducts
     training independently prior to the crew STX event, either in the motor pool, simulator
     system, or in a local training area. The primary trainers during the crew STX are the
     company commander with platoon sergeants and the Master Gunner providing oversight
     and assistance as necessary.

TRAIN THE TRAINERS—TABLE III

     3-49. Units execute the crew STX in a variety of ways. Typically, each platoon conducts
     training independently prior to the crew STX event, either in the motor pool, simulator
     system, or in a local training area. The primary trainers during the crew STX are the
     company commander with platoon sergeants providing oversight and assistance as
     necessary.

             Note. Much like crew platform evaluators, the evaluators for any crew
             exercise provide exceptional training for the evaluator, second only to
             executing the task with their organization. Units should dedicate sufficient
             time to certify the evaluators so they can provide mentorship to the training
             crew.

     3-50. To prepare the trainers, units provide—
          • Commander’s training objectives.
          • Scenario and lane concept with crew tasks.
          • Conduct of the range briefing for the training event.
          • Reviews of T&EOs and evaluation processes for all the tasks.
          • Risk assessments.
          • Identification of supporting doctrinal publications for use as primary
                references.
          • TADSS, pyrotechnics, blanks, and BESs used during the training event
                (see appendix A for the training munitions requirements for the complete
                training strategy).

ENGAGEMENT TASK AND PURPOSE

     3-51. The crews, leaders, and evaluators must understand the task and purpose of the
     engagements, the critical skills tasks, and how they are linked to their training. Each of
     the engagements should be reviewed to understand what they contain, the standards for
     each, and how they build from previous training and to the next event’s engagements.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  69

Chapter 3

RECONNAISSANCE OF THE SITE—TABLE III

     3-52. Commanders use an authorized training area or series of training areas for the
     Table III, STX. Commanders follow the installation’s policies and procedures for
     scheduling the training location as far in advance as practical. Once commanders have
     established the method of training and evaluation, they should secure the training
     location and appropriate TADSS, equipment, or systems that enhance the training event.

     3-53. Once scheduled, the commander, Master Gunner, platoon leaders and platoon
     sergeants conduct a reconnaissance of the training areas to—

          • Verify sufficient space is available to support the entire unit through multiple
                training lanes.

          • Ensure multiple iterations or rotations through the scenario can be achieved
                with appropriate retraining opportunities.

          • Ensure sufficient administrative areas are available to support group
                discussions, briefings, and AARs.

          • Provide sand table or terrain board.
          • Provide terrain board kits.
          • Verify the training area can support the unit’s training while in a chemical

                environment, based on the scenario design (protection.)
          • Verify casualty evacuation and medical evacuation notional capabilities in

                support of the training scenario, for which units mostly include—
                 Only frequency modulation reporting.
                 Requests for procedures for training purposes (sustainment).

ISSUE THE ORDER—TABLE III

     3-54. Commanders can issue an operation order (OPORD), warning order
     (WARNORD), or use the training schedule as necessary. The WARNORD should
     include the appropriate tasks, required leaders, and equipment needed, as well as the
     unit’s training timeline including walk-through, rehearsals, and AARs. WARNORDs
     for external organizations must include sufficient reaction time for the appropriate
     personnel and equipment needed.

     3-55. The OPORD or WARNORD should include the list of tasks that commanders
     evaluate during the event, and the method of evaluation. Commanders should provide
     sufficient time for subordinate leaders to train the tasks and skills listed in the order.
     Commanders should ensure the appropriate training resources are available and
     accessible. Upon receipt, subordinate leaders that have SOPs for their organizations
     must ensure their procedures support and complement the higher headquarters
     publication.

70  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025

              Table III—Situational Training Exercise

REHEARSE—TABLE III

     3-56. The unit uses all training resources, materials, and equipment available to conduct
     rehearsals of training events. During the rehearsal, commanders ensure their unit has
     sufficient time to train to the specified standard including AARs and retraining time.
     Commanders ensure their unit verifies optional scenarios that increase complexity when
     warranted.

     3-57. Commander uses the rehearsal as an opportunity to verify the tasks selected for
     Table III are consistent with the commander’s training guidance.

     3-58. Units should rehearse the training event from start to finish, and confirm—
          • The opposing forces understand the training scenarios.
          • Sufficient space is available to provide appropriate distances for offensive
                engagements.
          • Analog and digital systems are operational within the organization and to the
                higher headquarters regardless of their location.
          • Additional evaluator training support packages are complete and provide for
                multiple iterations.
          • Sufficient administrative areas are available.
          • A review of AAR capabilities.
          • Methods and actions required to provide fire support (notional).
          • Integration of attack aviation and close air support during the scenario (notional
                inclusion for call-for tasks).
          • Location of retraining for crews as necessary.

     3-59. Once the unit completes the walk-through and training rehearsal, the commander
     makes any necessary adjustments to the plan and issues fragmentary orders
     (FRAGORDs) as necessary.

EXECUTE TABLE III

     3-60. Once the unit arrives at the training location, they should—
          • Conduct a safety briefing for the training event.
          • Provide a conduct of the range briefing to establish the sequence of events for
                the training day.
          • Conduct sensitive item checks prior to executing training.
          • Assign evaluators as appropriate.
          • Issue the OPORD for the training mission.
          • Execute the training scenario.

     3-61. The training unit conducts informal AARs of each crew completing a phase of
     training within the training mission. During training, the assigned evaluators, executive
     officer, first sergeant (1SG), and master gunner review the subordinate unit’s
     performance. They follow the unit SOP, prescribed tactics, techniques, procedures, and
     the appropriate T&EOs, and review completed crew score sheets and roll-up
     documentation for clarity and completeness.

     3-62. Figure 3-13 on page 72 through figure 3-32 on page 91 provide the task,
     conditions, and standards for each engagement sequentially. The unit’s scenario
     sequence will vary based on the training area available, time, day and night mix of

Chapter 3

ASSESS—TABLE III

     3-73. The intent of STXs is to train the crews within the organization on the appropriate
     conduct of fire skills, various crew critical skills, and emergency procedures across two
     training days. Proper evaluation and assessment of crew performance is key to
     improving individual and crew skills prior to moving to the live-fire events.

     3-74. Units can maximize their AARs and assessments of training by fully integrating
     I-MILES CVTESS, HITS, laser target interface device, and CMU/IPU/CMUR systems
     in the training event. These systems used together provide a near-digital range facility
     capability at the company and battalion level.

FIRING GROUP AFTER ACTION REVIEWS

     3-75. For the firing group, each crew is given an AAR at the completion of the day phase
     and night phase by a qualified VCE. Use DA Form 8265-1 to record and track the results
     of each crew’s performance on each engagement at the completion of each phase.
     Tracking crew and platoon statistics on each engagement allow the unit leadership to
     identify areas for improvement.

             Note: VCEs must be certified or recertified as described in TC 3-20.31.
             Performing the duties as a VCE during the conduct of Table III, STX is a
             significant part of building experience and knowledge of those gunners and
             VCs without firing.

    3-76. Units should track the various firing information from the score sheets to  identify
    where in the conduct of fire crews and the overall unit should apply additional   training
    effort. Some of those indicators are—

         • Target acquisition, detection, and identification.
         • Time to fire.
         • Time to kill.
         • Opening and closing times for machine gun engagements.
         • Gunner lay errors.
         • Scanning techniques.
         • Engagement techniques.
         • Conduct of fire issues.
         • Penalties by type.

    3-77. Each crew should receive a formal AAR following the guidelines found in
    TC 3-20.31. Each evaluator should use DA Form 8265 and DA Form 8265-1 as the basis
    for their evaluation, identifying the firing occasional information from the score sheet
    timelines. The evaluator and VC each review and sign the DA Form 8265-1.

92         TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                   Table III—Situational Training Exercise

CRITICAL SKILLS TASK AFTER ACTION REVIEWS

     3-78. Evaluators should provide clear and articulate comments and insight about the
     positive and negative actions of the crews they observed. Evaluators should use the
     T&EOs to guide them through the evaluation process and use the doctrinal publications
     to support their comments.

     3-79. The evaluators should share the overall performance of the unit, including the
     observations, with the entire training unit. This allows the subordinate units to learn from
     other unit’s best practices, procedures, and tactics, and at times from their shortcomings
     or mistakes. The commander should identify crews within the organization that
     demonstrate superior performance.

     3-80. At the completion of this portion of crew Table III, STX, commanders, executive
     officers, and 1SGs should gain insight to where their unit’s SOPs are working and where
     they need additional attention. If time is available, commanders should update and
     provide those changes or revisions to the unit prior to executing any live-fire event. Of
     particular interest is the drivers’ training program, vehicle load plans, stowage of
     individual tactical gear, crew evacuation procedures, and medical evacuation
     procedures.

RETRAIN

     3-81. The unit Master Gunner maintains the unit roll-up by engagement. A
     subcategorized roll-up by platoons provides the commander a snapshot of training
     trends, strengths, and weaknesses. The Master Gunner should not only identify crews
     that did not achieve a passing standard, but also crews that struggled on specific
     engagement tasks. Once done, the unit can establish and conduct the appropriate focused
     retraining followed by a subsequent evaluation.

     3-82. All crews must successfully complete the course of fire based on the standards for
     the engagements listed earlier in this chapter. No crew may proceed to Table IV or any
     other live-fire event, including zeroing, or sub-caliber events until they have achieved
     the minimum standard on Table III.

     3-83. Platoon sergeants and platoon leaders should identify crews that require additional
     training on the various crew critical skills. Although the evaluation may be completed,
     units should integrate sustainment training into their unit training plans, particularly
     when environmental conditions change, movement during limited visibility is required,
     or planning or expecting tactical movement through restricted or rugged terrain.

DRIVER’S TRAINING—TABLE III

     3-84. Leaders should determine the number of miles driven during the day and night
     phases for their assigned drivers. This information should be recorded, including the
     miles driven, miles driven with the enhanced viewer, and any tactical (off-road) miles
     driven.

                                      Chapter 4

                       Table IV—Basic

    Table IV, Basic, is designed to train basic skills to enable proper leader and
    Soldier execution of those crew skills. This table builds upon those skills
    trained and tested during Table I, II, and III. It is a combination of primary
    armament screening or zero and machine gun zero procedures.
    The design of Table IV is to complete the screening and zeroing process of
    the primary armament and machine guns, complete main gun confirmation
    from the gunner and tank commander’s positions, and train and evaluate the
    crew’s ability to engage single and multiple stationary or moving targets
    placed in a tactical array with an operational or simulated degraded fire
    control system, as the conditions require. Crews execute these tasks from
    the defense using full-caliber ammunition.

             Note. Not all Abrams-series tanks will include a commander’s independent
             viewer or Common Remotely Operated Weapon Station (CROWS). For
             those variants, the VC will determine how to engage those targets.

OVERVIEW OF TABLE IV

     4-1. Table IV provides crews the ability to align their fire control system, screen or zero
     their ammunition by type, and complete basic machine gun engagement skills from a
     defensive firing position. The three task sets (or sections) to Table IV, Basic are—

          • Screening/zeroing.
          • Main gun confirmation
          • Basic machine gun engagements.

     4-2. Completion of the screening and zeroing procedures must occur prior to moving to
     the next task group in Table IV. This is to ensure the fire control system aligns correctly
     and the ammunition can hit the target as expected ballistically. The main gun
     confirmation is conducted after successfully completing the screening and zeroing task
     group. It provides confirmation the fire control system is operating correctly using
     power control from the gunner's and tank commander's stations. It includes a muzzle
     reference sensor (MRS) update between those two engagements. Lastly, the basic
     machine gun engagements provide training on target acquisition, target hand-off,
     commander's independent thermal viewer use, engagement techniques, and employing
     the CROWS.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  107

     conducting any task group within table IV. Failure to complete the preparation to fire
     tasks may result in a failure to successfully complete the tasks and engagements within
     Table IV and will cause significant loss of resources to the unit.

Chapter 4

             Note. Commanders who identify additional training needs for their crews
             prior to progressing to Table V can integrate additional engagements found
             in appendix D, using Table B, basic skills engagements. Commanders can
             select engagements from the training menu based on specific training gaps.
             When including additional engagements, the unit is responsible for
             coordinating and providing the necessary training resources.

PURPOSE OF TABLE IV

     4-6. Table IV, Basic, is a skills assessment and risk mitigation tool for the commander.
     It assists the commander in determining the safe and effective use of the platform, its
     weapons, identification of threats and common ammunition by the crewmembers in the
     unit. Further, it identifies shortfalls and training gaps in those gunnery specific skills,
     which build crew proficiency and unit readiness.

METHOD FOR TABLE IV

     4-7. Table IV includes the live fire accuracy screening test (LFAST) and machine gun
     zero procedures to ensure the accuracy of the tank before training initiates. It continues
     with a hands-on, performance-based evaluation of basic gunnery skills to the Army-
     standard of knowledge or skill in a live-fire environment. For the live-fire engagements,
     it utilizes experienced evaluators to educate, train, test, and retrain crews to meet or
     exceed those standards.

END STATE FOR TABLE IV

     4-8. The tank commander and gunner assigned as the crew have successfully
     demonstrated the skills and knowledge required to continue training on the tank through
     platform qualification. Commanders are confident in their Soldier's abilities in the
     position assigned.

     4-9. The evaluated engagements in Table IV provide the commander a means to verify
     the crew's basic weapons platform proficiency prior to further live-fire training. It is a
     guide for the commander to identify individual and crew weapons platform strengths
     and weaknesses on specific engagement techniques and employment of the vehicle’s
     fire control system capabilities and enablers. The commander and Master Gunner use
     the results from Table IV, Basic to structure the unit's crew training program.

TASK, CONDITIONS, AND STANDARD FOR TABLE IV

     4-10. The three task groups of Table IV, have their own unique set of task, conditions,
     and standards. The task, conditions, and standards for each task group are presented is
     sequential for clarity in the following sections of this chapter. The task groups within
     Table VI, Basic, are:

          • Task Group A, screening and zeroing.
          • Task Group B, main gun confirmation.
          • Task Group C, basic machine gun.

110  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                                    Table IV—Basic

PLAN—TABLE IV

     4-11. Table IV, Basic, is a live environment, live-fire event that uses large and small
     caliber ammunition. It will require the use of appended TADSS, and the use of BESs
     during the defensive engagements.

     4-12. This section details the resources needed for planning purposes common to all
     phases of Table IV. It includes general information on the following topics for
     consideration, which are the—

          • Time and throughput—the estimated time required to complete the training
                event including throughput considerations for planning.

          • Ammunition—the training ammunition requirements for phase of table IV,
                which includes a list of the required BESs to support the Army-standard target
                presentations for the defensive engagements.

          • Uniform—the minimum uniform requirements for the training event.
          • Facilities—available facilities that support the training event.
          • Range support—the administrative support requirements to run the live-fire

                range facility including evaluation support.
          • Facilities—the Army-standard facilities capable of supporting the training

                event to standard.
                 These are listed as primary and alternate facilities.
                 Facility type is based on inherent capabilities, availability, and

                     supportability.
          • Standard targets—specifically used for zeroing procedures for the main gun

                and machine guns, these targets are required to conduct Table IV.
          • Training plan—the minimum tasks, general guidelines, and special instructions

                for the training event.

     4-13. Table IV is a critical live-fire event. Failing to conduct the screening and zeroing
     procedures for the platform accurately will cripple any follow-on live-fire event
     outcomes.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  111


---

Chapter 4

                            PREP-TO-FIRE ACTIONS

                    Crews may not conduct Table IV, V, or VI without
                    completing the complete preparation to fire
                    (prep-to-fire) tasks. Failure to complete the
                    following actions to standard within 30-days of
                    conducting any live-fire engagement may cause
                    damage to equipment, serious injury, or death:
                    • Preventative maintenance checks and

                       services.
                    • Boresight.
                    • Plumb and synch.
                    • Armament accuracy checks.
                    • Gunnery checks.
                    • Recoil exercise.

TIME AND THROUGHPUT—TABLE IV

     4-14. The throughput expectation for Table IV is 14 crews over the course of two days.
     This includes one training day to conduct Table IV, Task Group A, screening and
     zeroing, followed by one training day for Task Group B, main gun confirmation, and
     Task Group C, basic machine gun engagements. When using multiple range facilities,
     units should begin Task Group B, main gun confirmation, as soon as practical on day
     one of training.

TADSS FOR TABLE IV

     4-32. This course requires the use of the CMU. Some installations may refer to this
     device as an IPU for all crews. The CMU is available on the digital range facility
     scheduled (if equipped). Coordination with the scheduled range facility manager will
     determine unit responsibilities for installation, troubleshooting, maintenance, and use of
     the devices ahead of the training day(s). If the range does not have a CMU available,
     unit may draw the CMUR from the TADSS warehouse. The CMUR differs from the
     CMU in that it stores all run data on a solid-state drive on the vehicle, for use with the
     provided AAR suite in the CMUR kit.

     4-33. The brigade commander must approve authorization for deviation from this
     requirement if TADSS are unavailable in writing. Units should focus on training and
     reinforcing the skills in the direct fire kill chain on this table specifically with their
     thermal optics, day and night to best prepare them for the follow-on training events.

Chapter 4

PREPARE—TABLE IV

     4-34. Units must coordinate for the appropriate training facility, equipment, targetry,
     training munitions, and TADSS (if desired) to support screening and zeroing. During
     the preparation phase, the unit should ensure the proofing team and other personnel
     receive training to standard to support their respective roles. This includes range officers
     in charge, range safety officers, and ammunition handlers.

TRAIN THE TRAINERS—TABLE IV

     4-35. Table IV requires experienced and trained personnel to support the range. The unit
     must also include the field maintenance team in their preparation for screening and
     zeroing.

Proofing Team

     4-36. Table IV, Task Group A, screening and zeroing requires an experienced proofing
     team in the event of a screening test failure. The unit Master Gunner provides training
     to the proofing team’s members on the topics listed below at a minimum. The topics to
     be trained are—

          • Preparation to fire checks.
          • Armament accuracy checks.
          • Gun tube technology.
          • Error budget.
          • Special gunnery checks.
          • M1 gunner’s quadrant.
          • Screening procedures.
          • Determination of mean point of impact (MPI).
          • DA Form 2408-4 (Weapon Record Data).
          • Fire control system overview.

     4-37. These overview classes are required to ensure the Master Gunner has sufficient
     support during the screening process, particularly with conduct of the range, procedures,
     and determining discrete CCFs.

     4-38. To prepare the trainers, units provide the—
          • Commander’s training objectives.
          • Conduct of the range briefing for the training event.
          • Scenario and lane concept with crew tasks for training.
          • Risk assessment.
          • TADSS, pyrotechnics, blanks, and BESs used during the training event (see
                appendix A for the training munitions requirements for the complete training
                strategy).

126  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                                    Table IV—Basic

Vehicle Crew Evaluators

     4-39. Typically, units have certified each VC and most gunners as VCEs prior to
     entering the live-fire density. Units must have certified evaluators to ensure the training
     objectives for the training table are set and to provide effective AARs to each crew.

     4-40. A trained and certified VCE team must be available to support prior to live-fire to
     evaluate a gunnery density correctly. Once certification is completed, the unit must
     ensure sufficient VCEs are available to support the live-fire event. Evaluations of crew
     gunnery always come from outside the firing platoon element, and for qualification
     purposes, VCEs external to the battalion are required.

     4-41. Depending on the size of the weapons training density (make-up gunnery, platoon,
     company, or battalion) and the type of range facility used, the number of evaluators may
     vary. Having the correct number and type of evaluators per firing range maximizes the
     unit’s throughput of firing vehicles on the range, reduce range downtime, and provide
     for the highest quality evaluations.

RECONNAISSANCE OF THE SITE

     4-42. Commanders use an authorized training facility as described earlier. Units may
     elect to use two separate training facilities to support all the Table IV task groups
     efficiently. Commanders follow the installation’s policies and procedures for formally
     scheduling the training location as far in advance as practical. See TC 3-20.31 for more
     in-depth information concerning range operations at the unit level.

     4-43. Once scheduled, the commander, Master Gunner, platoon leaders and platoon
     sergeants conduct a reconnaissance of the training areas to—

          • Verify sufficient space is available to support the entire unit through multiple
                training lanes.

          • Ensure multiple iterations or rotations through the scenario are achievable with
                appropriate retraining opportunities.

          • Ensure sufficient administrative areas are available to support group
                discussions, briefings, and AARs.

          • Provide sand table or terrain board.
          • Identify the ammunition supply point.
          • Determine the conduct of the range or the flow of training to maximize the

                live-fire range time.
          • Identify the range operating system and verify the training scenarios have been

                properly loaded.
          • Schedule proofing of the range, based on the higher headquarters OPORD.

ISSUE THE ORDER

     4-44. Commanders can issue an OPORD, WARNORD, or use the training schedule as
     necessary. The WARNORD should include the appropriate tasks, required leaders, and
     equipment needed, as well as the unit’s training timeline including walk-through,
     rehearsals, and AARs. WARNORDs for external organizations must include sufficient
     reaction time for the appropriate personnel and equipment needed.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  127

REHEARSE—TABLE IV

     4-45. The unit uses all training resources, materials, and equipment available to conduct
     rehearsals of training events. During the rehearsal, commanders ensure their unit has
     sufficient time to train to the specified standard.

     4-46. Units should rehearse the training event from start to finish, and confirm—
          • The proofing team understand the tasks, their role, and responsibilities.
          • Sufficient space is available to provide appropriate distances for engagements
                ranges and target dispersion.
          • Additional crew training packages are available, such as the following:
                 DA Form 8265.
                 DA Form 8265-12A (Crew Platform Data Record).
                 DA Form 8265-12B (Live Fire Accuracy Screening Test [LFAST]).
                 DA Form 8265-12C (Live Fire Accuracy Screening Test [LFAST]
                     Worksheet).
                 DA Form 1594 (Daily Staff Journal or Duty Officer’s Log) for tower logs.
          • Sufficient administrative areas are available.

     4-47. Once the unit completes the walk-through and rehearsal, the commander makes
     any necessary adjustments to the plan and issues FRAGORDs as necessary.

Chapter 4

SCENARIO REQUIREMENTS FOR LIVE-FIRE ENGAGEMENTS

     4-50. This table is designed to train and evaluate the crew’s ability to engage stationary
     or moving targets placed in a tactical array, from the defense, during day and limited
     visibility conditions.
     4-51. All engagements are evaluated using certified VCEs using DA Form 8265 and
     DA Form 8265-1 as the official record of training. Master Gunners must ensure the
     training records are included in each crew’s gunnery packet (see TC 3-20.31).
     4-52. The engagements listings are by capability type using the standard engagement
     naming structure listed in TC 3-20.31. It provides the requirements for each engagement,
     which are demonstrated in paragraphs 4-53 through 4-62.

Task Description—Table IV

     4-53. The task description includes both the engagement number (the table number plus
     engagement task) followed by the system capability trained.

Firing Vehicle Posture—Table IV

     4-54. The firing vehicle posture establishes the offense or defense condition for the
     engagement.

Firer—Table IV

     4-55. The firer column identifies which crewmember is to engage the target listed.
     Targets defeated by a different crewmember listed as the firer result in an auto-zero
     penalty, failing to adhere to course conditions.

Weapon (or Ammunition)—Table IV

     4-56. Identifies the weapon (coaxial, caliber .50, or primary armament ammunition type)
     that is used to defeat the threat. Design of each engagement is for a specific weapon or
     ammunition type (primary armament) for use, which relates to the crew conduct of fire,
     optic selection, engagement techniques, or other unique implied tasks. Crews that fail to
     announce the appropriate weapon or ammunition, or appropriate selection for
     subsequent or supplemental engagements may be penalized IAW TC 3-20.31.

Target Type—Table IV

     4-57. The target required for the presentation. Unless a specific, non-standard is used,
     all targetry requirements listed are found in TC 25-8.

Target Posture—Table IV

     4-58. The target posture establishes if the target is stationary, moving, or evasive for the
     engagement. See TC 3-20.31 for more information on target postures.

130  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                                    Table IV—Basic

Range (to Target)—Table IV

     4-59. The required range (full scale) from the firing vehicle to the target. For offensive
     engagements, this distance calculation is from the center of the movement box to the
     target. The range to target distance is provided in meters and is displayed as a whole
     number, such as 600.

     4-60. The target may be placed at the distance indicated, plus or minus 200 meters. For
     example, when the range to target is listed as 600 meters, the target may be placed
     between 400 meters and 800 meters. This allows flexibility designing the scenario on
     varying maneuver range facilities with different capabilities, target counts, emplacement
     locations, and terrain restrictions.

     4-61. No change to the EM is authorized. The range bands listed with their applied
     tolerances do not require any adjustment to the EM. Units are not authorized to exceed
     the target placement tolerances.

Engagement Modifier

     4-62. The EM is the time in seconds required to achieve 100 points against the listed
     threat. To defeat the threat successfully, the crew must achieve the kill standard against
     the target and have an overall engagement time of no more than the EM plus 20 seconds.
     See TC 3-20.31 for the evaluation, timing, and scoring procedures.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  131

Chapter 4

TABLE IV, TASK GROUP A—SCREENING AND
ZEROING

     4-65. The first task group to complete on Table IV is the screening of the tank following
     the Army calibration policy. Once complete with the screening and possible discrete
     CCF confirmation, the crew conducts machine gun zero for the coaxial machine gun and
     commander’s caliber .50 heavy machine gun.

     4-66. This task group ensures the crew can accurately align the tank’s optics, and that
     the platform’s fire control system is calculating an accurate ballistic solution for primary
     armament and coaxial machine gun engagements. Further, this ensures each sub-system
     of the fire control system is functioning properly after all non-firing preparation to fire
     (prep-to-fire) checks are complete.

     4-67. Screening and zeroing are an empirical means of tuning the fire control system
     with the ballistic solution. It requires that this tuning must take place at the distance (or
     range to target) of the greatest tactical interest. Boresighting and screening/zeroing
     should both be conducted at a range which corresponds to the most likely tactical
     engagement range.

                    Army-Standard Screening Distance

          The Army-standard screening distance to the ST-5 panel for all
          Abrams-series tanks has changed from 1,500 meters to 1,600
          meters, plus or minus 20 meters.
          This increase in range provides the ability to conduct screening or
          discrete zero procedures at a tactically relevant range to target and
          enables a live-fire precision alignment of the gunner’s auxiliary
          sight.

     4-68. Conduct of screening and zeroing is on a live-fire facility that can support primary
     armament as well as small arms surface danger zones. Crews will follow the procedures
     outlined in the vehicle’s technical manual using an ST-5 panel at 1,600 meters, plus or
     minus 20 meters.

     4-69. Master Gunners will record the strike of each primary armament round on the zero
     form and update DA Form 2408-4, where authorized by the commander, for each
     vehicle. Although the hard copy of the DA Form 2408-4 is in the vehicle’s equipment
     record folder, the Master Gunner should consider consolidating the forms to ensure
     accuracy of information during the gunnery density.

134  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                              Table IV—Basic

4-70. The task, conditions, and standards for Table IV, Task Group A, screening and
zeroing, is provided in figure 4-19. Master Gunners should note the change to the
screening panel range to target.

Figure 4-19. Table IV, Basic, screening and zero, task, conditions, and
                                           standard

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  135

Chapter 4

     4-71. Once crews have completed their prep-to-fire checks, boresighting, and other
     alignment procedures, they conduct the screening and zero procedures for their primary
     armament against an ST-5 screening target. The Master Gunner or designated
     experienced platoon sergeant is responsible for controlling the firing order, vehicle
     movement, and procedures for screening and zeroing from the vehicle technical manual.

     4-72. The proofing team will check any crew that fails the screening process. The unit
     Master Gunner and maintenance chief will determine the most probable cause for the
     screening failure and take appropriate measures to correct the deficiencies, whether
     mechanical, ammunition specific, or crew induced.

     4-73. Once the crew is complete with screening for all primary armament ammunition
     types, the crew completes coaxial and commander’s weapon station machine gun zero.
     Typically, the VC and gunner are responsible for the conduct of the machine gun zeroing
     process after receiving clearance to fire from the tower.

     4-74. As the crew completes the zeroing process and is prepared to conduct the next
     phase. Based on the conduct of the range, the crew will move to the designated holding
     area (firing order) and await instructions to proceed.

     4-75. The screening test consists of firing first sabot, then multi-purpose antitank
     (MPAT) target practice tracer ammunition at a screening test target at 1,600 meters
     (tactically relevant distance) (see figure 4-20). The target must be placed within plus or
     minus 20 meters of the required range.

     4-76. Screening procedures begin on an open range facility in a firing status. The tower
     follows the flow, as required by the actions and conduct of the crew and ballistic
     performance of the rounds fired.

     4-77. The following sections describe the actions executed for each phase of Table IV,
     Basic. They include a section for the LFAST, main gun confirmation, and basic machine
     gun engagements.

TABLE IV, TASK GROUP B—MAIN GUN
CONFIRMATION

     4-96. The second task group to complete is main gun confirmation. These are conducted
     only after successful completion of Task Group A, screening and zeroing by the crew.

PURPOSE

     4-97. Table IV, Basic, Task Group B, main gun confirmation, provides the crew a means
     to verify their ability to employ the fire control system against stationary and moving
     targets from a defensive firing position. The two live-fire engagements provide an
     evaluation of the crew’s conduct of fire and coordination, lethality against common
     threats, and ensure the fire control system operates as expected in normal mode. It also
     reinforces the crew’s use of the MRS update to make corrections to the ballistic solution
     to offset for uneven heating and cooling of the gun tube common during main gun firing.

METHOD

     4-98. The main gun confirmation engagements include three distinct steps: the gunner’s
     defensive main gun engagement, MRS update, and the tank commander’s defensive
     main gun engagement. Coupled with the LFAST and machine gun zero, completing
     Table B, main gun confirmation, builds crew confidence in the system, maintenance
     functions, crew coordination and cohesion, and berm drills.

END STATE

     4-99. The crew is confident in their fire control system, ballistic solutions, accuracy of
     the MRS update, tactical movement and engagements from a defensive position, and
     their crew coordination. The crew is confident in their conduct of fire process, driver
     actions, crew responses, and the ability to hit stationary and moving targets from the
     gunner’s and tank commander’s position with a fully mission capable system.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  175


---

Chapter 4

     4-100. Task Group B’s two live-fire engagements are the first of 10 evaluated
     engagements. The task, conditions, and standard for Task Group B, main gun
     confirmation, are shown in figure 4-58.

      Figure 4-58. Table IV B, main gun confirmation task, conditions, and
                                                standard

     4-101. Crews may only perform Task Group B, main gun confirmation, on the crew’s
     vehicle. It is designed to train and evaluate the crew’s ability to engage single and
     multiple stationary and moving targets placed in a tactical array, during the day or night,
     with a fully operational fire control system, as depicted in the task, conditions, and
     standard for each engagement. Crews execute these tasks from the defense using
     full-caliber training munitions.

     4-102. These engagements require the use of full-scale targetry as described in the
     planning section. The unit may increase the range to target to challenge the crew’s
     engagement skills as their training goals and objectives direct, however, the EM for each
     target may not change.

176  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025

DEFENSIVE FIGHTING POSITIONS

     4-103. As the tower is prepared to conduct the confirmation engagements from the
     defense, they will control the movement of all vehicles in the firing order. The tower
     will direct the crew to the appropriate defensive firing position and await further
     instructions.

     4-104. Once set, the tower directs the crew to MOVE TO RED STATUS, test fire their
     machine guns (if necessary), and report when set. As the crew completes the test firing,
     they report SET to the tower. As the conduct of the range allows, the tower directs the
     crew to conduct the engagements for evaluation. Units must enforce the appropriate
     firing position within the battle positions to reinforce their proper tactical use as shown
     in figure 4-59.

Engagement 43

     4-107. Engagement 43 is the gunner’s engagement and fired from the defense with a
     fully operational fire control system using power controls. This engagement is against a
     stationary tank at 1,400 meters and moving personnel carrier at 1,600 meters, as shown
     in figure 4-61. This verifies the gunner’s ability to engage multiple main gun threats,
     change ammunition type, and confirm the ballistic solution for sabot and MPAT rounds.
     The MPAT round is used on the moving target to provide the maximum amount of
     determined lead in the ballistic solution (lowest muzzle velocity projectile at distance
     while tracking a moving threat).

     4-108. This is required to be the first engagement. This ensures the fire control system
     and gunner’s power control handles are working as intended. This engagement is
     evaluated by the VCEs using DA Form 8265.

Chapter 4

TABLE IV, TASK GROUP C—BASIC MACHINE GUN

     4-116. The third task group to complete are the basic machine gun engagements. These
     are only conducted after successful completion of the screening, zeroing, and main gun
     confirmation tasks by the crew.

PURPOSE

     4-117. Table IV, Task Group C, basic machine gun, is a skills assessment and risk
     mitigation tool for the commander. It assists the commander in determining the safe and
     effective use of the platform, its weapons, identification of threats and common
     ammunition by the crewmembers in the unit. Further, it identifies shortfalls and training
     gaps in those gunnery specific skills, which build crew proficiency and unit readiness.

METHOD

     4-118. Task Group C is a hands-on, performance-based evaluation of basic machine gun
     engagement skills to the Army-standard of knowledge or skill in a live-fire environment.
     It utilizes experienced evaluators to educate, train, test, and retrain Soldiers to meet or
     exceed those standards.

END STATE

     4-119. The tank crew has successfully demonstrated the skills and knowledge required
     to continue training on the platform through platform qualification. Commanders are
     confident in their Soldiers’ abilities in the position assigned.

     4-120. The tasks evaluated in Task Group C provide the commander a means to verify
     the crew’s basic weapons platform proficiency prior to further live-fire training. It is a
     guide for the commander to identify individual and crew weapons platform strengths
     and weaknesses on specific engagement techniques and employment of the vehicle’s
     fire control system capabilities and enablers. The commander and Master Gunner use
     the results from Table IV, Basic, to structure the unit’s crew training program.

190  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                              Table IV—Basic

Task Group C, basic machine gun. Vehicles that fail to screen may not progress to any
live-fire table including the defensive engagements. The task, conditions, and standard
for Table IV, Task Group C, basic machine gun, are in figure 4-71.

   4-122. Crews should perform Task Group B on the crew’s vehicle. It is designed to train

   when ammunition is unavailable or to conduct retraining after live-fire training is
   complete.

Chapter 4

ASSESS—TABLE IV

     4-132. For Table IV, the screening and zeroing portion of the event is a maintenance
     and crew confidence exercise. The crew must ensure the fire control system is
     functioning as intended after all prep-to-fire procedures have been complete. In doing
     so, it provides the crew confidence in the procedures, policies, equipment, ammunition,
     and their own skill.

     4-133. The assessment of the live-fire engagements provides feedback to the crew and
     commander on the crew’s ability to tactically employ the tank against common threat
     scenarios. The feedback from these AARs identifies strengths and weaknesses within
     the crew and organization to address in future training plans.

LFAST CREW FEEDBACK

     4-134. The platoon sergeant or Master Gunner should provide an informal AAR for
     zeroing procedures that includes at a minimum:

          • Actions for each ammunition type.
          • Location of hits for each ammunition type.
          • Review of fixed biases, variable biases, and random errors that contribute to

                the accuracy of the round.
          • Zeroing procedures for each machine gun.
          • Methods to reduce round-to-round dispersion and crew error.

LIVE-FIRE ENGAGEMENTS CREW FEEDBACK

     4-135. Each crew should receive an informal AAR following the guidelines found in
     TC 3-20.31. Each evaluator will provide the general time to engage the threat, time to
     move to and from the enfilade firing position, and sensings for each target engaged. The
     evaluator should discuss the process of the MRS update and subsequent strikes of the
     round.

     4-136. Units should track the various firing information from the score sheets to identify
     where in the conduct of fire crews and the overall unit should apply additional training
     effort. Some of those indicators are—

          • Target acquisition, detection, and identification.
          • Time to fire.
          • Time to kill.
          • Opening and closing times for machine gun engagements.
          • Gunner lay errors.
          • Scanning techniques.
          • Engagement techniques.
          • Conduct of fire issues.

210  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                                    Table IV—Basic

RETRAIN—TABLE IV

     4-137. The unit Master Gunner maintains the crew screening and zeroing information.
     The company Master Gunner reviews the DA Form 8265-12 series documents, the tower
     log, and DA Form 2408-4 documents to identify crews that require additional training
     on prep-to-fire checks, armament accuracy checks, boresight device collimation,
     boresighting, plumb and synchronization, screening, or zeroing procedures.

     4-138. The unit Master Gunner maintains the unit roll-up by engagement. A
     subcategorized roll-up by platoons provides the commander a snapshot of training
     trends, strengths, and weaknesses. The Master Gunner should not only identify crews
     that did not achieve a passing standard, but also crews that struggled on specific
     engagement tasks. Once done, the unit can establish and conduct the appropriate focused
     retraining followed by a subsequent evaluation.

             Note. All crews should successfully complete the course of fire based on the
             standards for the engagements listed earlier in this chapter. No crew should
             proceed to Table V or any other live-fire event until they have achieved the
             minimum standard on Table IV. The commander and Master Gunner may
             assess the crews’ capability to proceed without qualifying the table based on
             target acquisition, target kills, fire commands and engagement techniques
             without achieving the minimum standard.

     4-139. Unit Master Gunners should include chair drills for the crews using the scenarios
     for each engagement for retraining purposes. Considerations include additional low-
     impact training drills to meet the demands of the 3 and 4 target engagements. Units
     should include future table’s engagements (Table V and Table VI, respectively) in the
     chair drills training to best prepare the crews for those events. Additional information
     on chair drills is in TC 3-20.31.

     4-140. Retraining for the live-fire engagements focus on the lowest scored engagements.
     Those provide the details during the direct fire kill chain that show where the training
     gaps exist.

     4-141. Units can retrain the failed engagements after addressing various corrective
     measures. Commanders may also select from the available list of basic engagement
     skills engagements in appendix D. The basic engagement skills engagements, called
     Table B, are not resourced. Units can use first round hit savings or other harvested
     munitions to self-resource those events for specific crews as the commander directs.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  211


---

Chapter 4

DRIVER RECORDS

     4-142. Leaders should determine the number of miles driven during the day and night
     phases for their assigned drivers. This information should be recorded, including the
     miles driven, miles driven with the enhanced viewer, and any tactical (off-road) miles
     driven. The miles driven build the driver’s experience base, mileage credit toward their
     Army-standard driver’s license requirements, and cumulative miles, hours, and
     assignment time for the expert driver’s badge for tracked vehicles.

SOLDIER INDIVIDUAL TASK PERFORMANCE

     4-143. Leaders track and train their Soldier’s individual and collective tasks trained and
     evaluated using tools provided by the ATN such as the SULT and the DJB. These
     systems interface directly with the DTMS to record training and evaluation results.

RECORD INDIVIDUAL TASK PERFORMANCE—TABLE IV

     4-144. Leaders track and train their Soldier’s individual and collective tasks trained and
     evaluated using tools provided by the ATN such as the SULT and the DJB. These
     systems interface directly with the DTMS to record training and evaluation results.

             Note. Leaders must be granted access by their commander or DTMS manager
             to enroll or assign their Soldiers the specific tasks from the ICTL that are
             trained and tested during the training event.

     4-145. From within the SULT, the authorized leader is enabled to view, maintain, and
     update—

          • Warrior tasks and battle drills.
          • MOS-specific individual critical tasks.
          • Unit-mandated training tasks.
          • Supporting T&EOs (access dependent on proponent distribution restrictions).

     4-146. Leaders should update their Soldier’s individual training records for training and
     evaluation of the following tasks, based on their assigned position. This is not an
     all-inclusive list of the trained and tested tasks for this event. Leaders should adjust
     based on the unit’s additions to the event.

     4-147. This ensures historical data is available throughout the Army force generation
     training cycle for the chain of command to assess strengths and weaknesses of crews
     and address these concerns in upcoming GST training. The most current GST records
     must accompany the unit or crew throughout the entire weapons training density.

UPDATING INDIVIDUAL TRAINING RECORDS

     4-148. Leaders update their SULT application with the appropriate training and
     evaluation outcomes for their crew members. The lists provided show the touch points
     associated with this training event.

     4-149. Tasks that are included within this event for the crew are found in figure 4-89
     through page 221. Leaders must determine if the tasks were effectively trained to the
     Soldier and the level of proficiency based upon their performance. Leaders then update
     the tasks for each Soldier based on their assessment.

                                      Chapter 5

                      Table V—Practice

    Crew platform, Table V, Practice, is a live-environment training event under
    live-fire conditions using full-caliber training ammunition, 3/4 scale
    realistic targetry and engagement scenarios, at a combat tempo on an
    authorized live-fire facility. Regardless of crew platform type, this table
    reinforces the basic skills trained during previous events but increases the
    tempo to expected engagement sequence capabilities to build leader and
    element tactical and technical mastery.
    This table includes execution of critical skills in a live environment. Table V
    prepares the crew to employ their weapons and systems against most
    common threat scenarios using prescribed targetry to a required standard.

             Note. This table requires the use of 3/4-scale targets to reinforce crew
             engagement speed and precision prior to qualification.

OVERVIEW OF TABLE V

     5-1. Table V, Practice, trains and evaluate the crew’s ability to engage stationary or
     moving targets placed in a tactical array, during day and limited visibility conditions,
     with an operational or simulated degraded fire control system, as applicable. Crews
     execute these tasks from the offense and defense using full-caliber ammunition against
     3/4 scale targets placed at full range.

     5-2. This table requires each crew to successfully complete the live-fire prerequisites,
     all zeroing procedures for their platform, and complete the defensive engagements on
     Table IV. This table is designed to train complex engagement skills, day and night, while
     fighting from the defense and during offensive engagements.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  223


---

Chapter 5

     5-3. Figure 5-1 shows where Table V, Practice, resides within the heavy tank training
     strategy through crew qualification. Table V is the first live-fire event where the crew
     conducts live-fire main gun engagements from the defense, as well as complex
     engagements while on the offense.

     Figure 5-1. Table V, Practice

224  TC 3-20.31-120                 31 July 2025


---

                                                                                                  Table V—Practice

PURPOSE OF TABLE V

     5-4. Table V is a skills assessment and risk mitigation tool for the commander. It assists
     the commander in determining the safe and effective use of the platform, its weapons,
     identification of threats and common ammunition by the crewmembers in the unit.
     Further, it identifies shortfalls and training gaps in those gunnery specific skills, which
     build crew proficiency and unit readiness.

METHOD FOR TABLE V

     5-5. Table V is a performance-based evaluation of basic and advanced gunnery skills to
     the Army-standard of knowledge or skill in a live environment under live-fire
     conditions. Conduct of Table V is on vehicle using full-caliber ammunition, and BESs,
     and 3/4-scale targetry at full-distance to provide the most challenging training prior to
     the qualification course of fire. It utilizes experienced evaluators to educate, train, test,
     and retrain Soldiers to meet or exceed those standards.

END STATE OF TABLE V

     5-6. The tank commander and gunner assigned as a crew have successfully
     demonstrated the skills and knowledge required to continue training on the platform
     through platform qualification. Commanders are confident in their Soldier’s abilities in
     the position assigned. The tasks evaluated in Table V provide the commander a means
     to certify the crew’s basic and advanced weapons platform proficiency prior to the
     qualification course of fire using full-caliber munitions. The commander and Master
     Gunner use the results from the qualification course when structuring the unit’s crew
     training program and is a readiness reportable item.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  225


---

Chapter 5

TASK, CONDITIONS, AND STANDARD OF TABLE V

     5-7. Outlined in figure 5-2 are the task, conditions, and standard for Table V, Practice.
     Units should not add to the live-fire requirements or tasks associate with Table V. If the
     commander is confident with the crew’s skills, they may increase the chemical,
     biological, radiological, and nuclear requirements from one day and one night
     engagement to suit their experience.

             the engagement is unqualified, regardless of overall engagement score.

PLAN FOR TABLE V

     5-8. Table V, Practice, is a live-fire training environment event using TADSS, and
     simulators, and includes the use of full-caliber and small arms training ammunition and
     BESs.

     5-9. This section details the resources needed for planning purposes. It includes general
     information on the following topics for consideration:

          • Uniform—the minimum requirements for the combat vehicle crewmember’s
                fire-retardant clothing and protective equipment.

          • Facilities—available facilities that support the training event.
          • Training plan—the minimum tasks, general guidelines, and special instructions

                for the training event.
          • Time and throughput—the estimated time required to complete the training

                event including throughput considerations for planning.
          • Ammunition—any training munitions required to complete the training event.
          • TADSS—any available training aids that directly support or augment the

                training event.

     5-13. The range support group provides VCE teams to evaluate the course of fire. This

     T-execution of the training event.
     5-14. The range support personnel general requirements are in figure 5-5. This includes

                              Table V—Practice

TRAINING PLAN

     5-15. Table V, Practice, provides a challenging scenario to the crews to prepare them
     for qualification and future collective training. It relies on engagements with a
     moderately higher level of difficulty that the qualification course of fire that exercises
     the crew’s conduct of fire and engagement skills. The increased difficulty in relation to
     the qualification course of fire is provided through:

          • Smaller targetry.
          • Increased number of targets.
          • Increased range to target for the majority of the engagements.
          • Greater dispersion between targets laterally and in depth.

     5-16. Units are required to use 3/4 scale targets at full range for the training scenarios
     unless specifically directed to use full-scale targetry (degraded engagements, for
     example). The Master Gunner has the option to use F-type rather than E-type silhouettes
     to increase the difficulty during machine gun engagements. (See TC 25-8, for additional
     information on scaled targetry.)

     5-17. This table prepares crews for the Table VI, Qualification. The table design is
     purposefully more difficult to master than the qualification. Units should retrain as
     necessary with full-caliber ammunition when possible. Units should use available
     resources to ensure all crews successfully complete Table V before proceeding to the
     qualification course of fire, Table VI.

Scenario Requirements for Table V

     5-18. This table is designed to train and evaluate the crew’s ability to engage stationary
     or moving targets placed in a tactical array, from the defense, during day and limited
     visibility conditions.

     5-19. Units must use 3/4 scale targets at full range for all scenarios except degraded
     engagements. Units may consider using full-scale for degraded engagements based on
     the units’ level of expertise. The Master Gunner may consider using F-type silhouettes
     rather than E-type silhouettes. (Refer to TC 25-8 for more information.)

     5-20. All engagements are evaluated using certified VCEs using DA Form 8265 and
     DA Form 8265-1 as the official record of training. Master Gunners must ensure the
     training records are included in each crew’s gunnery packet (see TC 3-20.31).

     5-21. The engagements listings are by capability type using the standard engagement
     naming structure listed in TC 3-20.31. The requirements for each engagement are listed
     in paragraphs 5-22 through 5-31.

Task Description—Table V

     5-22. The task description includes both the engagement number (the table number plus
     engagement task) followed by the system capability trained.

Firing Vehicle Posture—Table V

     5-23. The firing vehicle posture establishes the offense or defense condition for the
     engagement.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  231


---

Chapter 5

Firer—Table V

     5-24. The firer column identifies which crewmember is to engage the target listed.
     Targets defeated by a different crewmember listed as the firer result in an auto-zero
     penalty, failing to adhere to course conditions.

Weapon (or Ammunition)—Table V

     5-25. Identifies the weapon (coaxial, caliber .50, or primary armament ammunition type)
     that is used to defeat the threat. Design of each engagement is for a specific weapon or
     ammunition type (primary armament) for use, which relates to the crew conduct of fire,
     optic selection, engagement techniques, or other unique implied tasks. Crews that fail to
     announce the appropriate weapon or ammunition, or appropriate selection for
     subsequent or supplemental engagements may be penalized IAW the evaluation
     procedures in TC 3-20.31.

Target Type—Table V

     5-26. The target required for the presentation, IAW TC 25-8.

Target Posture—Table V

     5-27. The target posture establishes if the target is stationary, moving, or evasive for the
     engagement. See TC 3-20.31 for more information on target postures.

Range (to Target)—Table V

     5-28. The required range (full scale or equivalent scaled range) from the firing vehicle
     to the target. For offensive engagements, this distance calculation is from the center of
     the movement box to the target. The range to target distance is provided in meters and
     is displayed as a whole number, such as 600.

     5-29. The target may be placed at the distance indicated, plus or minus 200 meters. For
     example, when the range to target is listed as 600 meters, the target may be placed
     between 400 meters and 800 meters. This allows flexibility designing the scenario on
     varying maneuver range facilities with different capabilities, target counts, emplacement
     locations, and terrain restrictions.

     5-30. No change to the EM is authorized. The range bands listed with their applied
     tolerances do not require any adjustment to the EM. Units are not authorized to exceed
     the target placement tolerances.

Engagement Modifier—Table V

     5-31. The EM is the time in seconds required to achieve 100 points against the listed
     threat. To defeat the threat successfully, the crew must achieve the kill standard against
     the target and have an overall engagement time of no more than the EM plus 20 seconds.
     See TC 3-20.31 for the evaluation, timing, and scoring procedures.

232  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025

                                                                                                  Table V—Practice

     allowed to deviate from any target type, target posture, range, and weapon system or
     ammunition type for Table V, Practice.

TADSS—TABLE V

     5-35. This course requires the use of the CMU. The CMU is available on the digital
     range facility scheduled (if equipped). Coordination with the scheduled range facility
     manager will determine unit responsibilities for installation, troubleshooting,
     maintenance, and use of the devices ahead of the training day(s). If the range does not
     have a CMU available, unit may draw the CMUR from the TADSS warehouse. The
     CMUR differs from the CMU in that it stores all run data on a solid-state drive on the
     vehicle, for use with the provided AAR suite in the CMUR kit.

     5-36. The brigade commander must approve authorization for deviation from this
     requirement if TADSS are unavailable in writing. Units should focus on training and
     reinforcing the skills in the direct fire kill chain on this table specifically with their
     thermal optics, day and night to best prepare them for the follow-on training events.
     Figure 5-9 shows the TADSS required or recommended for Table V.

              Figure 5-9. Table V, Practice, TADSS

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120                        237
