Chapter 5

PREPARE FOR TABLE V

     5-37. Units must coordinate for the appropriate training facility, equipment, targetry,
     training munitions, TADSS (if desired), and pyrotechnics to support Table V. During
     the preparation phase, the unit should ensure the proofing team, VCEs, and other
     personnel receive training to standard to support their role as evaluators and trainers.
     This includes range officers in charge, range safety officers, and ammunition handlers.

TRAIN THE TRAINERS—TABLE V

     5-38. Typically, units have certified each VC and most gunners as VCEs prior entering
     the live-fire density. Units must have certified evaluators to ensure the training
     objectives for the training table are set and to provide effective AARs to each crew.

     5-39. Additional information on leader certification and train the trainer actions is found
     in TC 3-20.31. Units should review and plan for the appropriate training and
     certifications well in advance of the training event.

PREPARE VEHICLE CREW EVALUATORS

     5-40. A trained and certified VCE team must be available to support prior to live fire to
     evaluate a gunnery density correctly. After certification, the unit must ensure sufficient
     VCEs are available to support the live-fire event. Evaluations of crew gunnery always
     come from outside the firing platoon element, and for qualification purposes, VCEs
     external to the battalion are required.

     5-41. Depending on the size of the weapons training density (make-up gunnery,
     platoon, company, or battalion) and the type of range facility used, the number of
     evaluators may vary. Having the correct number and type of evaluators per firing range
     maximizes the unit’s throughput of firing vehicles on the range, reduce range downtime,
     and provide for the highest quality evaluations. See TC 3-20.31 for more in-depth
     information on VCEs, the evaluation team, and the evaluation process.

     5-42. To prepare the trainers, units provide—
          • Commander training objectives.
          • Conduct of the range briefing for the training event.
          • Scenario and lane concept with crew tasks for training.
          • Risk assessments.
          • Identification of supporting doctrinal publications for use as primary
                references.
          • TADSS, pyrotechnics, blanks, and BESs used during the training event (see
                appendix A for the training munitions requirements for the complete training
                strategy).

238  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                                  Table V—Practice

RECONNAISSANCE OF THE SITE FOR TABLE V

     5-43. Commanders use an authorized training facility for Table V, Practice.
     Commanders follow the installation’s policies and procedures for formally scheduling
     the training location as far in advance as practical. See TC 3-20.31 for more in-depth
     information concerning range operations at the unit level.

     5-44. Once scheduled, the commander, Master Gunner, platoon leaders and platoon
     sergeants conduct a reconnaissance of the training areas to—

          • Verify sufficient space is available to support the entire unit through multiple
                training lanes.

          • Ensure multiple iterations or rotations through the scenario are achievable with
                appropriate retraining opportunities.

          • Ensure sufficient administrative areas are available to support group
                discussions, briefings, and AARs.

          • Provide sand table or terrain board.
          • Identify the ammunition supply point.
          • Determine the conduct of the range or the flow of training to maximize the live-

                fire range time.
          • Identify the range operating system. Verify the training scenarios have been

                properly loaded.
          • Schedule proofing of the range, based on the higher headquarters OPORD.
          • Ensure requested TADSS are available if provided by the range facility.
          • Issue the order.

     5-45. Commanders can issue an OPORD, WARNORD, or use the training schedule as
     necessary. The WARNORD should include the appropriate tasks, required leaders, and
     equipment needed, as well as the unit’s training timeline including walk-through,
     rehearsals, and AARs. WARNORDs for external organizations must include sufficient
     reaction time for the appropriate personnel and equipment needed.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  239


---

Chapter 5

REHEARSE TABLE V

     5-46. The unit uses all training resources, materials, and equipment available to conduct
     rehearsals of training events. During the rehearsal, commanders ensure their unit has
     sufficient time to train to the specified standard including AARs and retraining time.
     Commanders ensure their unit verifies optional scenarios that increase complexity when
     warranted.

     5-47. Units should rehearse the training event from start to finish, and confirm—
          • The VCEs and proofing team understand the training scenarios and tasks.
          • Sufficient space is available to provide appropriate distances for engagements
                ranges and target dispersion.
          • Analog and digital systems are operational within the organization and to the
                higher headquarters regardless of their location.
          • Additional crew training packages (DA Form 8265, DA Form 8265-1, and
                appropriate zero documentation) are complete and provide for multiple
                iterations.
          • Sufficient administrative areas are available.
          • Review AAR capabilities, encouraging use of thru-sight video systems to
                increase the fidelity and quality of the AARs.
          • Location of retraining for crews as necessary.
          • Accessibility to crew simulator systems, plumb and synchronization ramps,
                tracking and manipulation panels, and additional bore sighting locations are
                available.

     5-48. Once the unit completes the walk-through and training rehearsal, the commander
     makes any necessary adjustments to the plan and issues FRAGORDs as necessary.

240  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                                  Table V—Practice

EXECUTE TABLE V

     5-49. Once the unit arrives at the training location, they should—
          • Conduct a safety briefing for the training event.
          • Provide a conduct of the range briefing to establish the sequence of events for
                the training day.
          • Conduct sensitive item checks prior to executing training.
          • Assign VCE support as appropriate.
          • Issue the OPORD for the training mission.
          • Execute the training scenario.

     5-50. The training unit conducts formal AARs of each crew completing a phase of
     training within the training mission. During training, the assigned VCEs, executive
     officer, 1SG, and Master Gunner review the subordinate unit’s performance. They
     follow the unit SOP, prescribed tactics, techniques, procedures, and the appropriate
     T&EOs, and review completed crew score sheets and roll-up documentation for clarity
     and completeness.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  241

ENGAGEMENT 57

     5-58. Engagement 57 is a degraded engagement where the gunner must engage multiple
     threats on the offense without a laser range finder using degraded gunnery techniques
     (see figure 5-24 and figure 5-25). This engagement challenges the crew by using
     degraded gunnery techniques and reinforces battlecarry and battlesight preparation to
     fire, battlesight gunnery, and advanced target acquisition with a mid- and long-range
     threat array. The CROWS is still operational for target acquisition and range
     determination.

     5-59. Engagement 58 is a degraded engagement for the gunner to apply the gunner’s

     gun threats (see figure 5-26 and figure 5-27). This engagement includes the required use
     of manual controls. The gunner must clear the gun-target line effectively and control the
     movement of the vehicle once clear. The tank commander may not use their power
     control handle or the commander’s independent thermal viewer’s (CITV) designate
     function to place the gunner near the threat. The CROWS is still operational for target

Chapter 5

ASSESS TABLE V

     5-61. The intent of the practice course of fire is to train the crews within the organization
     on the appropriate conduct of fire skills in both offensive and defensive postures against
     common threat arrays using the primary armament, coaxial machine gun, and
     commander’s machine gun. Proper evaluation and assessment of crew performance is
     key to improving individual and crew skills prior to moving to the qualification course
     of fire.

     5-62. Each crew should receive a formal AAR following the guidelines found in
     TC 3-20.31. Each evaluator should use DA Form 8265 and DA Form 8265-1 as the basis
     for their evaluation, identifying the firing occasional information from the score sheet
     timelines. The evaluator and VC each review and sign the DA Form 8265-1.

             Note. VCEs must be certified or recertified as described in TC 3-20.31.
             Performing the duties as a VCE during the conduct of Table V, Practice is a
             significant part of building experience and knowledge of those gunners and
             VCs without firing.

     5-63. Units should track the various firing information from the score sheets to  identify
     where in the conduct of fire crews and the overall unit should apply additional   training
     effort. Some of those indicators are—

          • Target acquisition, detection, and identification.
          • Time to fire.
          • Time to kill.
          • Opening and closing times for machine gun engagements.
          • Gunner lay errors.
          • Scanning techniques.
          • Engagement techniques.
          • Conduct of fire issues.
          • Penalties by type.

262        TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                                  Table V—Practice

RETRAIN—TABLE V

     5-64. The unit Master Gunner maintains the unit roll-up by engagement. A
     subcategorized roll-up by platoon provides the commander a snapshot of training trends,
     strengths, and weaknesses. The Master Gunner should not only identify crews that did
     not achieve a passing standard, but also crews that struggled on specific engagement
     tasks. Once done, the unit can establish and conduct the appropriate focused retraining
     followed by a subsequent evaluation.

     5-65. All crews successfully complete the course of fire based on the standards for the
     engagements listed earlier in this chapter.

             Note. All crews should successfully complete the course of fire based on the
             standards for the engagements listed earlier in this chapter. No crew should
             proceed to Table VI or any other live-fire event until they have achieved the
             minimum standard on Table V. The commander and Master Gunner may
             assess the crews’ capability to proceed without qualifying the table based on
             target acquisition, target kills, fire commands, and engagement techniques.

     5-66. Unit Master Gunners should include chair drills for the crews using the scenarios
     for each engagement for retraining purposes. Units should include future table’s
     engagements in the chair drills training to best prepare the crews for those events.
     Additional information on chair drills is in TC 3-20.31.

DRIVER’S TRAINING—TABLE V

     5-67. Leaders should determine the number of miles driven during the day and night
     phases for their assigned drivers. This information should be recorded, including the
     miles driven, miles driven with the enhanced viewer, and any tactical (off-road) miles
     driven. The miles driven build the driver’s experience base, mileage credit toward their
     Army-standard driver’s license requirements, and cumulative miles, hours, and
     assignment time for the expert driver’s badge for tracked vehicles.

                                      Chapter 6

                  Table VI—Qualification

    Crew platform, Table VI, Qualification, is the record course of fire used to
    determine the live-fire proficiency of a crew platform. Table VI serves as
    the live-fire proficiency event for readiness reporting. It serves as the
    live-fire prerequisite for the crew to move to collective live-fire events.
    Table VI, Qualification, is a live environment-training event under live-fire
    conditions using full-caliber training ammunition, realistic targetry and
    engagement scenarios, at a combat tempo, conducted on an authorized
    live-fire facility. Regardless of crew platform type, this table reinforces the
    basic skills trained during previous events but increases the tempo to
    expected engagement sequence capabilities to build leader and element
    tactical and technical mastery.

OVERVIEW OF TABLE VI

     6-1. Table VI, Qualification, is from the crew’s vehicle and uses full caliber training
     ammunition on an authorized live-fire facility or safety certified training area that
     assesses an element’s tactical employment proficiency and lethality. Table VI trains and
     evaluate the crew’s ability to engage stationary or moving targets placed in a tactical
     array, during day and limited visibility conditions, with an operational or simulated
     degraded fire control system, as applicable. Crews execute these tasks from the offense
     and defense using full-caliber ammunition.

     6-2. This table requires each crew to successfully complete the live-fire prerequisites,
     all zeroing procedures for their platform, and complete the defensive engagements on
     Table VI. This table is designed to train and evaluate complex engagement skills, day
     and night, while fighting from the defense and during offensive engagements. It is the
     qualification event for heavy tank crews.

                              Table VI, Qualification

          This event is an external evaluation for qualification purposes.
          Failing indicates a clear lack of proficiency at basic engagement
          skills.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  273


---

Chapter 6

     6-3. Figure 6-1 shows where Table VI, Qualification, resides withing the heavy tank
     training strategy through crew qualification. Table VI is the qualification event where
     the crew is evaluated on their demonstrated proficiency executing critical live-fire main
     gun, machine gun, and simultaneous engagements from the offense and defense, during
     the day and night phases. It requires the crew to demonstrate their ability to effectively
     employ the heavy tank’s weapons using fully mission capable fire control system
     components as well as degraded engagements.

     Figure 6-1. Table VI, Qualification

274  TC 3-20.31-120                       31 July 2025


---

                                                                                          Table VI—Qualification

PURPOSE OF TABLE VI

     6-4. Table VI is a skills assessment and risk mitigation tool for the commander and is
     the platform’s qualification for reporting purposes. It is also a proficiency gate the crew
     must successfully complete prior to entering any collective live-fire event. It assists the
     commander in determining the safe and effective use of the platform, its weapons,
     identification of threats and common ammunition by the crewmembers in the unit.
     Further, it identifies shortfalls and training gaps in those gunnery specific skills, which
     build crew proficiency and unit readiness.

METHOD—TABLE VI

     6-5. Table VI is a performance-based evaluation of basic and advanced gunnery skills
     to the Army-standard of knowledge or skill in a live environment under live-fire
     conditions. Conduct of Table VI is on vehicle using full-caliber ammunition, and BESs.
     It utilizes experienced evaluators to educate, train, test, and retrain Soldiers to meet or
     exceed those standards.

END STATE—TABLE VI

     6-6. The VC and gunner assigned as a crew have successfully demonstrated the skills
     and knowledge required to continue training on the platform through platform
     qualification. Commanders are confident in their Soldier’s abilities in the position
     assigned. The tasks evaluated in Table VI provide the commander a means to certify the
     crew’s basic and advanced weapons platform proficiency prior to collective live-fire
     events. The commander and Master Gunner use the results from the qualification course
     when structuring the unit’s crew training program and is a readiness reportable item.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  275


---

TASK, CONDITIONS, AND STANDARD—TABLE VI

     6-7. Outlined in figure 6-2 are the task, conditions, and standard for Table VI,

     Table VI. The division commander or equivalent maintains the qualification course of

PLAN

     6-8. Table VI, Qualification, is a live-fire training environment event using TADSS and
     includes the use of full-caliber and small arms training ammunition and BESs. The
     qualification course of fire requires external VCEs and coordination with the higher
     headquarters for support.

     6-9. This section details the resources needed for planning purposes. It includes general
     information on the following topics for consideration, which are—

          • Uniform—the minimum requirements for the combat vehicle crewmember’s
                fire-retardant clothing and protective equipment.

          • Facilities—available facilities that support the training event.
          • Training plan—the minimum tasks, general guidelines, and special instructions

                for the training event.
          • Time and throughput—the estimated time required to complete the training

                event including throughput considerations for planning.
          • Ammunition—any training munitions required to complete the training event.
          • TADSS—any available training aids that directly support or augment the

                training event.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  277

     6-10. Crews must be in the appropriate vehicle crewmember uniform or prescribed unit

     Figure 6-3 provides the minimum uniform training requirements this event.

RANGE SUPPORT FOR TABLE VI

     6-12. The range support group provides VCE teams to evaluate the course of fire. This

     T-execution of the training event.
     6-13. The range support personnel general requirements are in figure 6-5. This includes

280  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025

                                                                                          Table VI—Qualification

TRAINING PLAN FOR TABLE VI

     6-14. Table VI, Qualification, provides a challenging scenario to the crews to prepare
     them for collective live-fire events and deployment. The qualification course exercises
     the crew’s conduct of fire and engagement skills in a demanding manner that
     demonstrates the minimum proficiency of the crew.

     6-15. Units are required to use full-scale targets at full range for the engagement
     scenarios unless specifically directed. To maintain training standards across the Army,
     there is no authorization for deviation from the engagements on Table VI beyond the
     guidelines listed in TC 3-20.31 for alternate targetry.

     6-16. The design of Table VI is purposefully challenging to the crews while providing
     realistic engagements that exercise the crew’s ability to utilize the key capabilities of the
     vehicle’s fire control system. Units retrain as necessary with full-caliber ammunition
     using first-round hit savings (harvested ammunition) from the previous tables in the
     training strategy. Units must manage the available resources to ensure all crews
     successfully complete Table VI before proceeding to any collective live-fire event or
     deployment.

     6-17. All crew platform tables are “multiple qualification criteria” tables. Crews must
     score at least 70 points per target according to the target kill standards in TC 3-20.31 on
     single and multiple target engagements to qualify the engagement. Crews must also
     score more than 700 points overall with a minimum of 70 points per target during 7 of 10
     engagements on the qualification table to be used. Crews must qualify at least one
     engagement during day and one engagement during limited visibility to be qualified.

     6-18. Table VI uses full caliber training ammunition on an authorized live-fire facility
     or safety certified training area that assesses an element’s tactical employment
     proficiency and lethality.

     6-19. Crews must successfully complete the qualification course prior to executing any
     collective live-fire training. Units must follow the training guidelines for those gates in
     the appropriate TC. Units may conduct “make-up” qualifications prior to live collective
     training when required but units must accomplish live-fire prerequisites as directed.

Scenario Requirements for Table VI

     6-20. This table is designed to train and evaluate the crew’s ability to engage stationary
     or moving targets placed in a tactical array, from the defense, during day and limited
     visibility conditions.

     6-21. All engagements are evaluated using certified VCEs using DA Form 8265 and
     DA Form 8265-1 as the official record of training. Master Gunners must ensure the
     training records are included in each crew’s gunnery packet (see TC 3-20.31).

     6-22. The engagements listings are by capability type using the standard engagement
     naming structure listed in TC 3-20.31. It provides the requirements for each engagement,
     which are listed in paragraphs 6-23 through 6-32.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  281


---

Chapter 6

Task Description—Table VI
     6-23. The task description includes both the engagement number (the table number plus
     engagement task) followed by the system capability trained.

Firing Vehicle Posture—Table VI
     6-24. The firing vehicle posture establishes the offense or defense condition for the
     engagement.

Firer—Table VI
     6-25. The firer column identifies which crewmember is to engage the target listed.
     Targets defeated by a different crewmember listed as the firer result in an auto-zero
     penalty, failing to adhere to course conditions.

Weapon (or Ammunition)—Table VI
     6-26. Identifies the weapon (coaxial, caliber .50, or primary armament ammunition type)
     that is used to defeat the threat. Design of each engagement is for a specific weapon or
     ammunition type (primary armament) for use, which relates to the crew conduct of fire,
     optic selection, engagement techniques, or other unique implied tasks. Crews that fail to
     announce the appropriate weapon or ammunition, or appropriate selection for
     subsequent or supplemental engagements may be penalized IAW TC 3-20.31.

Target Type—Table VI
     6-27. The target required for the presentation, IAW TC 25-8.

Target Posture—Table VI
     6-28. The target posture establishes if the target is stationary, moving, or evasive for the
     engagement. See TC 3-20.31 for more information on target postures.

Range (to Target)—Table VI
     6-29. The required range (full scale or equivalent scaled range) from the firing vehicle
     to the target. For offensive engagements, this distance calculation is from the center of
     the movement box to the target. The range to target distance is provided in meters and
     is displayed as a whole number, such as 600.
     6-30. The target may be placed at the distance indicated, plus or minus 200 meters. For
     example, when the range to target is listed as 600 meters, the target may be placed
     between 400 meters and 800 meters. This allows flexibility designing the scenario on
     varying maneuver range facilities with different capabilities, target counts, emplacement
     locations, and terrain restrictions.
     6-31. No change to the EM is authorized. The range bands listed with their applied
     tolerances do not require any adjustment to the EM. Units are not authorized to exceed
     the target placement tolerances.

282  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                          Table VI—Qualification

Engagement Modifier—Table VI
     6-32. The EM is the time in seconds required to achieve 100 points against the listed
     threat. To defeat the threat successfully, the crew must achieve the kill standard against
     the target and have an overall engagement time of no more than the EM plus 20 seconds.
     See TC 3-20.31 for the evaluation, timing, and scoring procedures.

Engagements—Table VI

     6-33. Figure 6-6 on page 284 shows the breakdown of what target type, target posture,
     range and weapon system or ammunition type used for each task number. Units are not
     allowed to deviate from any target type, target posture, range, and weapon system and
     ammunition.

TADSS—TABLE VI

     6-36. This course requires the use of the CMU. The CMU is available on the digital
     range facility scheduled (if equipped). Coordination with the scheduled range facility
     manager will determine unit responsibilities for installation, troubleshooting,
     maintenance, and use of the devices ahead of the training day(s). If the range does not
     have a CMU available, unit may draw the CMUR from the TADSS warehouse. The
     CMUR differs from the CMU in that it stores all run data on a solid-state drive on the
     vehicle, for use with the provided AAR suite in the CMUR kit.

     6-37. The brigade commander must approve authorization for deviation from this
     requirement if TADSS are unavailable. Units should focus on training and reinforcing
     the skills in the direct fire kill chain on this table specifically with their thermal optics,
     day and night to best prepare them for the follow-on training events. Figure 6-9 shows
     the TADSS required or recommended for Table VI.

Figure 6-9. Table VI, qualification, training aids, devices, simulators, and
                                 simulations requirements

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  287

Chapter 6

PREPARE FOR TABLE VI

     6-38. Units must coordinate for the appropriate training facility, equipment, targetry,
     training munitions, TADSS (if desired), and pyrotechnics to support Table VI. During
     the preparation phase, the unit should ensure the proofing team, VCEs, and other
     personnel receive training to standard to support their role as evaluators and trainers.
     This includes range officers in charge, range safety officers, and ammunition handlers.

TRAIN THE TRAINERS—TABLE VI

     6-39. Typically, units have certified each VC and gunner as VCEs prior entering the
     live-fire density. Units must have certified evaluators to ensure the training objectives
     for the training table are set and to provide effective AARs to each crew.

     6-40. Additional information on leader certification and train the trainer actions is found
     in TC 3-20.31. Units should review and plan for the appropriate training and
     certifications well in advance of the training event.

VEHICLE CREW EVALUATORS—TABLE VI

     6-41. A trained and certified VCE team must be available to support prior to live fire to
     evaluate a gunnery density correctly. After certification, the unit must ensure sufficient
     VCEs are available to support the live-fire event. Evaluations of crew gunnery always
     come from outside the firing platoon element, and for qualification purposes, VCEs
     external to the battalion are required.

     6-42. Depending on the size of the weapons training density (make-up gunnery, platoon,
     company, or battalion) and the type of range facility used, the number of evaluators may
     vary. Having the correct number and type of evaluators per firing range maximizes the
     unit’s throughput of firing vehicles on the range, reduce range downtime, and provide
     for the highest quality evaluations. See TC 3-20.31 for more in-depth information on
     VCEs, the evaluation team, and the evaluation process.

     6-43. To prepare the trainers, units provide—
          • Commander training objectives.
          • Conduct of the range briefing for the training event.
          • Scenario and lane concept with crew tasks for training.
          • Risk assessments.
          • Identification of supporting doctrinal publications for use as primary
                references.
          • TADSS, pyrotechnics, blanks, and BESs used during the training event (see
                appendix A for the training munitions requirements for the complete training
                strategy).

288  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                          Table VI—Qualification

RECONNAISSANCE OF THE SITE—TABLE VI

     6-44. Commanders use an authorized training facility for Table VI, Qualification.
     Commanders follow the installation’s policies and procedures for formally scheduling
     the training location as far in advance as practical. See TC 3-20.31 for more in-depth
     information concerning range operations at the unit level.

     6-45. Once scheduled, the commander, Master Gunner, platoon leaders and platoon
     sergeants conduct a reconnaissance of the training areas to—

          • Verify sufficient space is available to support the entire unit through multiple
                training lanes.

          • Ensure multiple iterations or rotations through the scenario are achievable with
                appropriate retraining opportunities.

          • Ensure sufficient administrative areas are available to support group
                discussions, briefings, and AARs.

          • Provide sand table or terrain board.
          • Identify the ammunition supply point.
          • Determine the conduct of the range or the flow of training to maximize the

                live-fire range time.
          • Identify the range operating system and verify the training scenarios have been

                properly loaded.
          • Schedule proofing of the range, based on the higher headquarters OPORD.
          • Ensure requested TADSS are available if provided by the range facility.

ISSUE THE ORDER—TABLE VI

     6-46. Commanders can issue an OPORD, WARNORD, or use the training schedule as
     necessary. The WARNORD should include the appropriate tasks, required leaders, and
     equipment needed, as well as the unit’s training timeline including walk-through,
     rehearsals, and AARs. WARNORDs for external organizations must include sufficient
     reaction time for the appropriate personnel and equipment needed.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  289


---

Chapter 6

REHEARSE—TABLE VI

     6-47. The unit uses all training resources, materials, and equipment available to conduct
     rehearsals of training events. During the rehearsal, commanders ensure their unit has
     sufficient time to train to the specified standard including AARs and retraining time.
     Commanders ensure their unit verifies optional scenarios that increase complexity when
     warranted.

     6-48. Units should rehearse the training event from start to finish, and confirm—
          • The VCEs and proofing team understand the training scenarios and tasks.
          • Sufficient space is available to provide appropriate distances for engagements
                ranges and target dispersion.
          • Analog and digital systems are operational within the organization and to the
                higher headquarters regardless of their location.
          • Additional crew training packages [DA Form 8265, DA Form 8265-1, and
                appropriate CCFs (zero) documentation] are complete and provide for multiple
                iterations.
          • Sufficient administrative areas are available.
          • Review AAR capabilities. Use of thru-sight video systems is encouraged to
                increase the fidelity and quality of the AARs.
          • Location of retraining for crews as necessary.
          • Accessibility to crew simulator systems, plumb and synchronization ramps,
                tracking and manipulation panels, and additional bore sighting locations are
                available.

     6-49. Once the unit completes the walk-through and training rehearsal, the commander
     makes any necessary adjustments to the plan and issues FRAGORDs as necessary.

290  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                          Table VI—Qualification

EXECUTE TABLE VI

     6-50. Once the unit arrives at the training location, they should—
          • Conduct a safety briefing for the training event.
          • Provide a conduct of the range briefing to establish the sequence of events for
                the training day.
          • Conduct sensitive item checks prior to executing training.
          • Receive briefing from the external VCE team.
          • Establish the firing order.
          • Identify the maintenance support area for any fire control or weapons issues on
                the range.
          • Install TADSS as required.

     6-51. The training unit conducts informal AARs of each crew completing a phase of
     training within the training mission. During training, the commander, executive officer,
     1SG, and Master Gunner review the crew’s performance. They follow the unit SOP,
     prescribed tactics, techniques, procedures, and the appropriate T&EOs, and review
     completed crew score sheets and roll-up documentation for clarity and completeness.

             Note. The sequence of the engagements as well as which engagements are
             fired in a chemical environment are determined as unit policy directs.

Chapter 6

ASSESS TABLE VI

     6-62. The intent of the qualification course of fire is to train the crews within the
     organization on the appropriate conduct of fire skills during offensive and defensive
     postures against common threat arrays using the primary armament, coaxial machine
     gun, and commander’s machine gun. Proper evaluation and assessment of crew
     performance is key to improving individual and crew skills prior to moving to collective
     live-fire events.

     6-63. Each crew should receive a formal AAR following the guidelines found in
     TC 3-20.31. Each evaluator should use DA Form 8265 and DA Form 8265-1 as the basis
     for their evaluation, identifying the firing occasional information from the score sheet
     timelines. The evaluator and VC each review and sign the DA Form 8265-1.

             Note. Certified VCEs externally evaluate Table VI, Qualification. Units
             coordinate with their higher headquarters for those personnel. Typically, the
             brigade combat team will train and certify those evaluators. If there is no
             external unit that can meet the requirements for the primary evaluator,
             brigade coordination outside the division may be required to provide the
             appropriate personnel to evaluate the crews (see TC 3-20.31). When that level
             of support is not feasible, the division commander (or equivalent) must
             authorize self-evaluation using senior subject matter experts.

     6-64. Units should track the various firing information from the score sheets to  identify
     where in the conduct of fire crews and the overall unit should apply additional   training
     effort. Some of those indicators are—

          • Target acquisition, detection, and identification.
          • Time to fire.
          • Time to kill.
          • Opening and closing times for machine gun engagements.
          • Gunner lay errors.
          • Scanning techniques.
          • Engagement techniques.
          • Conduct of fire issues.
          • Penalties by type.

312        TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025

                                                                                          Table VI—Qualification

RETRAIN TABLE VI

     6-65. The unit Master Gunner maintains the unit roll-up by engagement. A
     subcategorized roll-up by platoons provides the commander a snapshot of training
     trends, strengths, and weaknesses. The Master Gunner should not only identify crews
     that did not achieve a passing standard, but also crews that struggled on specific
     engagement tasks. Once done, the unit can establish and conduct the appropriate focused
     retraining followed by a subsequent evaluation.

     6-66. All crews must successfully complete the course of fire based on the standards for
     the engagements listed earlier in this chapter. No crew should proceed to a collective
     live-fire event until they have achieved the minimum standard on Table VI,
     Qualification.

     6-67. Unit Master Gunners should include chair drills for the crews using the scenarios
     for each engagement for retraining purposes. Additional information on chair drills is in
     TC 3-20.31.

     6-68. Any crew that disqualified for any reason may not re-fire without brigade
     commander approval after corrective training is complete. See Chapter 7 for additional
     information on disqualified crews.

DRIVER’S TRAINING FOR TABLE VI

     6-69. Leaders should determine the number of miles driven during the day and night
     phases for their assigned drivers. This information should be recorded, including the
     miles driven, miles driven with the enhanced viewer, and any tactical (off-road) miles
     driven. The miles driven build the driver’s experience base, mileage credit toward their
     Army-standard driver’s license requirements, and cumulative miles, hours, and
     assignment time for the expert driver’s badge for tracked vehicles.

RECORD INDIVIDUAL TASK PERFORMANCE—TABLE VI

     6-70. Leaders track and train their Soldier’s individual and collective tasks trained and
     evaluated using tools provided by the ATN such as the SULT and the DJB. These
     systems interface directly with the DTMS to record training and evaluation results.

             Note. Leaders must be granted access by their commander or DTMS manager
             to enroll or assign their Soldiers the specific tasks from the ICTL that are
             trained and tested during the training event.

     6-71. From within the SULT, the authorized leader is enabled to view, maintain, and
     update—

          • Warrior tasks and battle drills.
          • MOS-specific individual critical tasks.
          • Unit-mandated training tasks.
          • Supporting T&EOs (access dependent on proponent distribution restrictions).

     6-72. Leaders should update their Soldier’s individual training records for training and
     evaluation of the following tasks, based on their assigned position. This is not an

                                      Chapter 7

                 Qualification Standards

    The crew tables culminate in the evaluation of the crew’s ability to destroy
    threat targets using full caliber ammunition both day and night.
    Qualification on Table VI is a prerequisite for crews to advance to collective
    live-fire training.
    Crew Table VI, Qualification, is the record course of fire used to determine
    the live-fire proficiency of a crew platform. Table VI serves as the
    commander’s assessment of lethality and proficiency of the crew platform.
    This qualification is required by AR 350-1 and described as a metric of unit
    readiness within AR 220-1. Crew platform qualification is the cornerstone
    of company, battalion or squadron, and brigade combat team readiness.

OVERVIEW OF TABLE VI

     7-1. Crew Table VI, Qualification, is the Army’s standard demonstration of a crew’s
     employment proficiency the crew platform during a live-fire event. Crews must
     successfully complete Table VI, Qualification, to progress to higher echelon live-fire
     collective events. Crew qualification is also used as prerequisites for additional training,
     certifications, and functional courses.

     7-2. All crew platform tables include multiple qualification criteria to determine when a
     crew is qualified. The multiple qualification criteria are not waiverable on the
     qualification table. The qualification requirements are discussed in the next sections.

                                   Qualified Crew

          A qualified crew is a VC and gunner combination that successfully
          completes crew platform, Table VI, Qualification and have not met
          any of the condemnation criteria of the qualification period.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  323


---

Chapter 7

TERMS OF REFERENCE

     7-3. Units must be familiar with the training terminology used to describe the various
     components of proficiency, qualification, and readiness. The following terms are used
     within this appendix when discussing qualification.

QUALIFICATION

     7-4. A qualified crew is a VC and gunner combination that successfully completes crew
     platform, Table VI, Qualification and have not met any of the condemnation criteria of
     the qualification period.

QUALIFICATION DURATION

     7-5. The length of time a qualification is valid provided all other qualification conditions
     exist. For crew platforms, the qualification duration is 12months.

PREREQUISITES

     7-6. Training events prior to live-fire that must be successfully completed by the Soldier
     or crew. Official records of the training must be maintained for a minimum of 12 months.

EXTERNAL EVALUATION

     7-7. Qualification events require external evaluations by certified VCEs. Evaluators are
     typically external to the battalion or squadron (see figure 7-1). For some units in remote
     locations, the unit must conduct the evaluations with trained leaders within the
     organization. In those circumstances, the evaluator for any crew should not have
     influence over the promotion potential or be in any professional competition with the
     VC or gunner.

                     Figure 7-1. External evaluations, crew platforms

QUALIFICATION PERIOD

     7-8. The period that begins the day a qualification is awarded and continues for
     365 days. This term is used to describe the time since qualification occurred.

CONDEMNATION CRITERIA

     7-9. The rules that determine if any given qualification is still valid. For crew platforms,
     these rules are time, key leader turn over, and commander assessment.

MULTIPLE-QUALIFICATION CRITERIA

     7-10. The rules used to crew platform live-fire to determine their qualification status or
     rating based on their performance during Table VI’s engagements.

324  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                         Qualification Standards

QUALIFICATION STANDARDS

     7-11. Crew Table VI, Qualification, is the record course of fire used to determine the
     live-fire proficiency of a crew platform. Table VI serves as the commander’s assessment
     of lethality and proficiency of the crew platform. This qualification is required by
     AR 350-1 and described as a metric of unit readiness within AR 220-1. Crew platform
     qualification is the cornerstone of company, battalion or squadron, and brigade combat
     team readiness.

     7-12. Crew Table VI, Qualification, is the Army’s standard demonstration of a crew’s
     employment proficiency the crew platform during a live-fire event. Crews must
     successfully complete Table VI, Qualification, to progress to higher echelon live-fire
     collective events. Crew qualification is also used as prerequisites for additional training,
     certifications, and functional courses. Certified, experienced leaders external to the unit
     evaluate crew platforms on the qualification course of fire.

     7-13. Crew qualification uses multiple-qualification criteria to determine the live-fire
     proficiency. To qualify on Table VI, the qualification course of fire, the crew must at a
     minimum successfully:

          • Qualify 7 of 10 engagements by achieving a minimum of 70 points on all
                targets presented within the engagement overall.

          • Score a minimum of 700 points out of a possible 1,000 points, overall.
          • Qualify a minimum of one day engagement and one night engagement.

     7-14. With proper training and adherence to the training strategy, units should achieve
     qualification on the first run attempt (termed “first run”) on a minimum of 90 percent of
     their crews.

                              First Run Qualification

          Units that fail to achieve 90 percent qualified crews on their first run,
          which is often referred to as Q1 indicate a failure to train the
          necessary skills at the company and troop level.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  325


---

Chapter 7

     7-15. The qualification standards for heavy tank crews are listed in figure 7-2. These
     standards establish the reporting requirements for crew platforms for readiness
     reporting.

     Figure 7-2. Crew platform qualification standards

326  TC 3-20.31-120                                     31 July 2025


---

                                                                                         Qualification Standards

CREW PLATFORM RATINGS

     7-16. Crew evaluation basis is on their level of competence on their platform using the
     standard evaluation procedures as described in the platform’s training publication. The
     end state for each crew after completing Table VI is a crew that is confident and
     competent to a standard that best prepares them for combat.

     7-17. The crew rating is an overall snapshot of the performance on the crew qualification
     table that provides commanders an overview of the crew’s performance and competence
     employing their platform. It is an indicator of unit readiness and training deficiencies
     for future training events.

     7-18. Crews will receive an overall qualification rating for Table VI, Crew
     Qualification, based on the multiple-qualification criteria as outlined in figure 7-3.
     Commanders are authorized to award crewmembers the appropriate weapons
     qualification badge based on the live-fire proficiency event rating including the
     corresponding qualification clasp.

Figure 7-3. Crew platform qualification badges authorized

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120                               327

Chapter 7

     7-19. When units require multiple attempts to qualify, units traditionally classify them
     by the maximum number of iterations of any engagement on the course of fire as
     described in figure 7-4.

                       Figure 7-4. Qualifying with multiple attempts

     7-20. The “Q#” ratings are used to identify weak performing crews that require
     additional training to achieve the appropriate readiness level expected of their MOS,
     assigned position, and unit readiness. Beyond the crew’s score, Q# ratings provide a
     rapid indication to the commander about where training deficiencies or gaps are present.

            Note. For an example of the Q# rating, a crew refires the 62 engagement 3
            times on Table VI to achieve the required 700 minimum points with 7
            qualified engagements. As the 62 engagement was fired multiple times (three
            in this case), the crew is rated qualified as Q3.

     7-21. Units that train according to the training strategy throughout the training period
     with proactive leadership involvement will achieve the desired training outcomes. These
     units typically achieve the benchmarks listed in figure 7-5.

328  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                              Qualification Standards

 Figure 7-5. Training benchmarks achieved by following the training strategy

QUALIFICATION RE-FIRES

     7-22. If a crew fails to qualify, they must re-fire select engagements for qualification.
     The highest rating that a crew can earn when re-firing is QUALIFIED.

     7-23. A crew re-fires only the engagements that they failed to achieve the minimum
     standard. Units should initially focus on those nonqualified engagements that provide
     the most points to gain. A crew re-fires only the number of engagements required to
     obtain a qualified rating.

     7-24. AR 220-1 and AR 350-1 delineates the qualification standards reporting
     requirements. They relate directly to the individual and collective training proficiency
     goals of the readiness model found in AR 525-29.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  329


---

Chapter 7

DISQUALIFICATION

     7-25. A disqualification is the immediate removal from live training of any unsafe crew
     until the crew satisfactorily completes a recertification program. Disqualification is a
     critical failure in the safe conduct of the range by leaders using weapons or weapon
     systems in such a manner that has the potential to injure or kill Soldiers or civilians, or
     excessive damage to government property.

     7-26. The following is a list of disqualification criteria:
          • A crew that performs any actions listed in the “immediate disqualification”
                section of DA Form 8265-1.
          • A crew that performs three each 30-point penalties as listed on
                DA Form 8265-1 during the same table.
          • A crewmember or entire crew can be disqualified and require recertification
                for actions not associated with firing.
                 The unit commander may disqualify individuals or crews that consistently
                     perform unsafe acts during non-firing or preparation tasks.
                 This type of disqualification will be at the discretion of the unit
                     commander.

     7-27. Any crew disqualified for any reason may not re-fire without brigade commander
     approval after corrective training is complete. Corrective training for disqualified crews
     includes, at a minimum, repeating and successful completion of:

          • Table I, GST.
          • Table II, STX-V. GTLF on the authorized training system.
          • Table III, STX.

     7-28. Disqualified crews indicate a significant lack of awareness for safety on the
     vehicle. The requirement for retraining on Tables I, II, and III, at a minimum, may be
     waived by the first general officer in the chain of command and may not be delegated.

             Note. The battalion or squadron commander is the approving authority for all
             recertification. A commander may impose additional retraining requirements
             as desired to ensure the crew’s safety and effective performance during
             live-fire training.

330  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                         Qualification Standards

SUSTAINING CREW PROFICIENCY

     7-29. Once the element has successfully completed their qualification event, the unit
     must consider how the condemnation criteria impact their subordinate’s proficiency.
     The following section describes how crew platforms maintain their proficiency, how to
     identify when a proficiency rating has triggered one or more condemnation criteria, and
     any options the commander can consider to lessen or mitigate those impacts.

CREW PLATFORM CONDEMNATION CRITERIA

     7-30. Qualifications remain valid until one of the condemnation criteria is met. The
     qualification condemnation criteria for crew platforms are noted in paragraphs 7-31
     through 7-34.

Time

     7-31. Twelve months is the maximum allowable time before recertification. This is the
     maximum qualification duration for any qualification, certification, or proficiency
     rating.

Key Leaders

     7-32. The minimum identified key leaders on the crew platform (tank commander and
     gunner for the heavy tank) are no longer assigned to their qualified position.

Commander Assessment

     7-33. Any commander within the chain of command determines the crew no longer
     maintains crew platform proficiency for any reason. Any condemnation based on the
     commander’s assessment will instigate immediate retraining.

                          NO ALTERATIONS TO THE
                          CONDEMNATION CRITERIA

                                    AUTHORIZED

                    Units may not alter, change, or modify the
                    condemnation criteria. Evaluation commanders have
                    the option, based on their assessment of the
                    element, to condemn any proficiency rating they are
                    authorized to assign.

     7-34. Once any crew loses their qualification status that crew must be removed from the
     battle roster. Once removed from the battle roster, commanders and their advisors
     review the potential to maintain the remaining crew members on either the crew roster
     or cross-train roster based on each situation.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  331


---

Chapter 7

TALENT MANAGEMENT

     7-35. Commanders can mitigate the impacts to their unit’s proficiency in a variety of
     methods. Talent management skills before, during, and after conducting the crew
     platform training strategy is required by leaders to ensure the maximum proficiency is
     maintained and sustained within the larger organization. Crew platform qualification has
     a direct impact on the unit’s overall readiness and contribute to the unit’s level of
     proficiency.

     7-36. To best manage crew turbulence when establishing crew rosters, the commander
     and staff consider the following process outlined in paragraphs 7-37 through 7-46 on
     page 334.

Identify

     7-37. Identify high performing Soldiers that demonstrate technical and tactical
     understanding who are prepared for increased responsibility. Consider their previous
     assignments and experience for potential assignment as a gunner or VC. Identify the
     Soldier’s year month available or date of expected return from overseas assignment for
     permanent change of station. Determine those Soldiers eligible for stabilization
     following established Army personnel policies.

Assess

     7-38. Units must assess new Soldier and crewmember proficiency on their platform.
     Commanders that receive new leaders who achieved proficiency within the last
     qualification period have assessment options to maintain element qualifications.
     Commanders can assess two previously qualified key leaders from different positions
     (tank commander or gunner) to determine their proficiency status if they are assigned
     together as a crew. This is the least preferred method of talent management and requires
     the commander to make assessments using previous crew records, sustainment training
     in simulations, and other assessments to determine their proficiency status.

Assign

     7-39. Select the tank commander/gunner combinations together. This maximizes the key
     leader’s longevity and future proficiency ratings more stable. Assign each crewmember
     to their position on their potential and longevity. Place those leaders in the next higher
     position (gunner to VC, or driver to gunner, for example).

Transfer

     7-40. When promotions occur, commanders should make every effort to keep the trained
     key leaders together, including inter-battalion transfers. Commanders should consider
     moving the newly promoted key leader and key leader combination together where the
     gaining unit within the battalion will benefit from maintaining proficient key leaders.

332  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                         Qualification Standards

Cross-train

     7-41. Experienced Soldiers are easier to train than new Soldiers. Units should utilize
     harvested training ammunition to train and qualify alternate firers to build experience.
     Units should invest first-round-hit ammunition savings, commonly referred to as
     ammunition harvesting, to cross training young leaders in key leader positions. In doing
     so, commanders can mitigate key leader turnover with trained and proficient leaders in
     stride with key leader turbulence. Ammunition available through first round hit savings
     vary from training event to training event. Units must be prepared to execute training
     based on the varying training munitions availability and plan for additional time on
     range facilities to build depth in their trained and qualified cross-trained crews.

Stabilize

     7-42. Commanders must ensure the personnel officer is involved in crew management
     through requesting stabilization for Soldiers or organizations in unique situations. Some
     of these situations are described in paragraphs 7-43 to 7-45.

Functional Course Graduation

     7-43. Soldier attendance to the various platform mastery courses or instructor courses
     provide units with a graduate stabilization capability. These types of requests maximize
     the unit’s investment in the Soldier’s by effectively stabilizing the Soldier with the unit
     for up to 24 months.

Stop Move

     7-44. Units request to stabilize specific Soldiers, small unit, or an entire organization six
     months prior to latest arrival date. This does not apply to Soldiers already on assignment
     instructions. The stop move is effective 180 days prior to the unit's latest arrival date and
     continues through redeployment to at least 90 days.

     7-45. The special actions branch at human resources command provides oversight and
     action against all stabilization, stop movement, and crew stabilization requests. For units
     that have an assigned latest arrival date the brigade commander requests stabilization
     for those VC and gunner combinations they foresee becoming turbulent in the next four
     quarters. For additional information, see the human resources command website.

Plan

     7-46. Commanders with the assistance of their Master Gunner must anticipate future
     turbulence before it happens. As the key leader turbulence periods occur (permanent
     change of station, date of expected return from overseas assignment, reenlistment
     options, promotion potential, and so forth), the commander must be able to visualize and
     identify the most common causes, their impacts on proficiency and readiness, and
     identify the most probable points of failure in sustaining readiness.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  333


---

Chapter 7

CREW PLATFORM TURBULENCE

     7-47. The commander may consider a turbulent crew as qualified (proficient) in extreme
     circumstances. Commanders accept the risk when assigning turbulent crewmembers
     together and carry that crew as qualified for reporting purposes. It is the least preferred
     method of crew management and is approved by the brigade commander.

     7-48. The commander should review the VC and gunner have displayed cohesion and
     proficiency on critical skills on their platform that build experience and proficiency
     without live-fire and—

          • Previously qualified in their assigned position on a different crew within the
                previous qualification period (12 months).

          • Successfully completed Table I, GST, for the unit within the previous
                qualification period or on a diagnostic exam.

          • Completed the simulations training strategy from entry-level through the GTLF
                exercise and typically takes between 8 and 12 hours in the simulator with a
                qualified instructor/operator and platoon sergeant oversight.

          • Successfully completed the crew platform, Table II, STX V GTLF with a score
                of 850 or above.

     7-49. If commanders are not completely confident in the crew cohesion, capability,
     experience, and performance, they must rate them as unqualified the next training
     density.

334  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025

Chapter 7

     7-55. The commander must understand what they can, should, and should not do to
     mitigate crew turbulence. When assessing turbulent crews, the commander should make
     use of the authorized simulations systems, GSTs, the VCE course, and other training
     assets. See the turbulent crew section in this chapter for additional information.

              Assignments with Qualified Rating Approval
                                       Authority

          Commanders should not carry turbulent crews without previous live
          fire demonstrated proficiency in position. Providing as much trigger
          time on the system as possible, as often as possible, will create less
          difficult talent management decisions.

          Carrying a turbulent crew as qualified is a commander option. The

                                     Appendix A

                         Ammunition

    The heavy tank ammunition requirements, capabilities, and description are
    outlined within this appendix. The ammunition provided is for training
    purposes.

AMMUNITION REQUIREMENTS

     A-1. The Abrams heavy tank crews complete the following events as indicated within
     this training circular. The actions listed below are in T-week for active component and
     T-month for reserve component concept. Frequency of execution of the training strategy
     is twice per fiscal year for active component and once per training year for reserve
     component. The events are—

          • Table I—GST should be completed between T-6 and T-X (execution) of a
                live-fire event.

          • Table II—Simulations should be completed between T-6 and T-X (execution)
                of a live-fire event, wherein each crew is authorized one hour per month with
                an authorized simulations system.

          • Table III—STX should be completed between T-6 and T-X (execution) of a
                live-fire event, and units should conduct Table III as concurrent training on all
                other listed Tables.

          • Table IV—Basic should be completed prior to Table V.
          • Table V—Practice should be completed prior to Table VI.
          • Table VI—Qualification, for which the duration is 12 months.

     A-2. The ammunition requirements for Tables III through VI is listed by table on
     figure A-1 on page 342. The requirements may not match DA Pam 350-38 (Standard in
     Weapons Training), which is commonly referred to as STRAC or Standards in Training
     Commission, which is the authorization. The unit Master Gunner must determine the
     annual forecast of ammunition required to meet the training goals and objectives of the
     commander. The Master Gunner uses the total ammunition management information
     system, often referred to as TAMIS, to review, validate, and submit unit ammunition
     requirements and forecast their training ammunition authorizations by month.

Appendix A

120MM AMMUNITION COMPONENTS

     A-6. All 120mm ammunition cartridges consist of four basic components, the case
     base, cartridge case, propellant, and projectile. See figure A-5 for more information.

                       Figure A-5. 120mm ammunition components

346  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                                                         Ammunition

CASE BASE

     A-7. The case base houses the primer element and supports the rear seal which provides
     rear obturation. This holds the combustible cartridge case with a spring disc pinned
     around the primer base thread assembly. The markings on the case base identify the
     nomenclature, lot number, primer lot number, and round type. See figure A-6 for
     detailed view of the case base.

              Figure A-6. Case base

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120         347


---

Appendix A

CASE BASE AND PRIMER

     A-8. The primer, as shown in figure A-7, is screwed into the case base. This receives
     the electrical charge from the circuit which ignites the primer filler. The primer filler is
     a volatile igniter made of benite strands that ignite the propellant.
     A-9. Benite is extruded strands of black powder (potassium nitrate, charcoal, and
     sulfur) embedded in nitrocellulose. When the electrical charge is applied to the ignitor,
     the ignitor actuates the benite strands which vent through the primer case holes causing
     deflagration of the propellant. Activation of the ignitor occurs between 280 mA
     (milliamp) and 1.25A (amps).

     Figure A-7. Case base and primer

348  TC 3-20.31-120                    31 July 2025


---

                                                                                                         Ammunition

CARTRIDGE CASE

     A-10. The cartridge case houses the propellant and the projectile. The cartridge case is
     locked with the case base, case seal, and the forward obturator. The forward obturator,
     located at the font of the cartridge case, causes forward obturation. Forward obturation
     is the action of the cartridge case expanding against the front portion of the chamber
     region by the high pressure of the internal and forward gases (see figure A-8).

                                    Figure A-8. Cartridge case

PROPELLANT

     A-11. The propellant is contained within the cartridge case (see figure A-9). The
     different propellant types vary by the model number of the round, listed later within this
     appendix.

              Figure A-9. Propellant

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120          349


---

Appendix A

PROJECTILE

     A-12. The projectile is the cylindrical portion of the cartridge between the end of the
     primer tube and the end of the cartridge case that is fitted with a tracer assembly to the
     rear. The projectile travels down the bore toward the target. The projectile, based on the
     type of round, may house a sub-projectile, payload, or explosive chemical energy
     compound and fuze.
     A-13. Based on the type of round, after shot exit, the round may—

          • Arm a chemical warhead.
          • Begin separating sabot petals from a sub-projectile.
          • Continue down range on the ballistic path.
     A-14. The projectile has three different color applications: black, olive drab, or light
     blue. The black projectile is an armor defeating round, olive drab is for anti-personnel
     or anti-material, and the light blue color indicates training (target practice). See
     figure A-10 for an illustration of the projectile location.

                                       Figure A-10. Projectile

350  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025

                                     Appendix B

         Tracking and Manipulation Boards

    T&MBs are tools to train hand-and-eye, coordination, gun control, speed,
    and accuracy using the platform’s fire control system. In this appendix, there
    are six variations as examples for units to consider; however, units can
    create unique layouts, as their training program requires.
    T&MBs, more commonly referred to as snake boards, should have a wide
    variety of designs with horizontal, vertical, and oblique motion in any
    direction. The panels may also incorporate (along the panel’s tracking path)
    scaled targets that replicate the most effective way to lay on, lase, and
    engage a target. Paths on the boards can be tracked in all fire control modes
    and incorporate the use of all sights in most cases.

OVERVIEW OF SNAKE BOARDS

     B-1. Use tracking and manipulation board exercises to augment the STX-V or use
     when no simulator system is available or accessible to the crew. T&MBs are locally
     manufactured based on the unit need. Completion of the tracking board exercise is the
     least preferred method of completing Table II for live fire.

     B-2. It should only be used in extreme cases (new vehicle with no simulator system,
     unit is deployed, or retraining is necessary in an austere or field environment) or as
     opportunity training in the motor pool, training area, or admin area on a range facility.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  357


---

Appendix B

     B-3. The purpose of crew platform, Table II, STX-V, is to ensure the crew possesses
     the skills and experience necessary to execute live-fire events and exercises safely,
     which implies a functional understanding of the engagement process, fire commands,
     engagement techniques, and lethality. This is the culminating test of their abilities and
     skills on their platform in simulation after completing the appropriate simulations
     training plan (see figure B-1).

                                    Figure B-1. Table II, STX-V

     B-4. Table II, STX-V, is a mandatory prerequisite for any live-fire event, including
     zero procedures, sub caliber or in-bore training event, or any live-fire maintenance
     procedure within the crew training strategy. Complete Table II between T-6 and T-week
     to support the training events.

     B-5. Units use the T&MB when training in austere environments or when simulation
     systems are unavailable or unacceptable. This appendix provides general information on
     the purpose and use of the T&MB.

358  TC 3-20.31-120  31 July 2025


---

                                                                         Tracking and Manipulation Boards

PURPOSE

     B-6. Table II, STX-V, is a skills assessment and risk mitigation tool for the
     commander. It assists the commander in determining the safe and effective use of the
     platform, its weapons, identification of threats and common ammunition by the
     crewmembers in the unit. Further, it identifies shortfalls and training gaps in those
     gunnery specific skills, which build crew proficiency and unit readiness.

METHOD

     B-7. Table II is a hands-on, performance-based evaluation of basic gunnery skills to
     the Army-standard of knowledge or skill in a simulator system (crew shelter). It utilizes
     experienced evaluators to educate, train, test, and retrain Soldiers to meet or exceed
     those standards.

END STATE

     B-8. The VC and gunner assigned as a crew have successfully demonstrated the skills
     and knowledge required to continue training on the platform through platform
     qualification. Commanders are confident in their Soldier’s abilities in the position
     assigned.

     B-9. The tasks evaluated in Table II provide the commander a means to certify the
     crew’s basic weapons platform proficiency prior to live-fire training. It is used as a guide
     for identifying individual and crew weapons platform strengths and weaknesses on
     specific engagement techniques and employment of the vehicle’s fire control system
     capabilities and enablers.

     B-10. The goal of this training is for the firer to reach the level of expertise with the
     system at which the firer anticipates movements of the sight and can maintain the point
     of aim while tracking. Examples of tracking boards are in appendix B with sample
     scripts for use and construction requirements. The tracking board exercise increase the
     level of expertise of the firer to manipulate the weapon system rapidly and without
     hesitation, commonly referred to as switchology.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  359


---

Appendix B

TASK, CONDITIONS, AND STANDARD—SNAKE BOARDS

     B-11. Figure B-2 provides the action, conditions, and standard when using the T&MBs
     for Table II.

     Figure B-2. Table II, STX-T&MBs

360  TC 3-20.31-120                   31 July 2025


---

                                                                         Tracking and Manipulation Boards

SCENARIO REQUIREMENT CONSIDERATIONS

     B-12. When using the T&MBs to meet Table II testing, units should use no less than 10
     separate movements to another panel across the array, complete the series of movements
     on each panel before assessing the gunner’s performance. As the layout, design, and
     functioning will vary, units must create their own timing and evaluation procedures,
     however, the standards listed in Figure B-2 should not change.

     B-13. When used as the GTLF on Table II, the unit should maintain a record of the
     crew’s performance and evaluator notes. The method the unit records the training is up
     to the design and script developed for training. It should include information like the
     administrative data on DA Form 8265, including crew roster, evaluator name, date of
     training, to name a few.

     B-14. Units should provide enough scorecards and performance measures for the
     evaluators. Maintain these records in the crew’s gunnery folder for 12 months.

DESIGN

     B-15. Platoon sergeants, the 1SG, or the unit Master Gunner will provide the VC
     training using the primary sight or independent thermal viewer as appropriate. Use of
     manual controls is not required for the VC’s exercise.

     B-16. The panels are constructed using 4-foot by 8-foot plywood, target cloth, or other
     suitable material. Tracking lines are 1-inch-wide black borders with a 2-inch-wide inner
     white path. Multiple panels can be aligned together by placing the start-point and
     endpoint of each panel at the height halfway point on the left and right side of the panel.

     B-17. The design of the panel track is up to the unit. Example panel designs are in
     figures B-3 on page 362 through B-9 on page 366. Variations should include—

          • Vertical track.
          • Horizontal track.
          • Right oblique to left oblique (diagonal movement).
          • Left oblique to right oblique (diagonal movement).
          • Random directional changes.
          • Targetry—removable, adjustable.
          • Switchology indicators—removable, adjustable.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  361

SCENARIO EXAMPLE

     B-21. Figures B-12 through B-23 on page 380 provide an example scenario using the

EXECUTION

     B-24. Evaluators utilize the unit provided criteria scoring checklist for the ten steps of
     the tracking and manipulation board exercise. The evaluators will use specified prompts
     and evaluators may not assist the crew to perform any function.

     B-25. Once testing is complete, the evaluator records the results on a locally created
     roll-up form. As each Soldier finishes or the time limit is up, whichever occurs first, the
     evaluator checks the Soldier’s performance as either GO or NO-GO, informs them of
     their performance on that task, and directs them to the next station or to retraining.
     B-26. The company Master Gunner or designated NCOIC, is responsible for completing
     unit and maintaining platoon and company roll-up information ensuring the next higher
     headquarters is informed, if required.

                                     Appendix C

            Deployed/Austere Qualification

    The austere qualification event, called Table A, is a record course of fire
    used to determine the live-fire proficiency of a crew platform. Table A
    serves as the live-fire proficiency event for readiness reporting for units
    requiring crew qualification in extreme circumstances while deployed. It is
    used by exception only, with permission from the first general officer in the
    chain of command.
    It serves as the live-fire prerequisite for the crew to move to collective
    live-fire events. Table A, Austere Qualification, is a live
    environment-training event under live-fire conditions using full-caliber
    training ammunition, austere targetry at half distance (1/2-scale range to
    target) and engagement scenarios, at a combat tempo, conducted on an
    authorized live-fire facility.
    This table reinforces the basic skills trained during previous events but is
    tailored for use on non-standard, host nation live-fire training facilities that
    do not meet the scenario requirements in Chapter 6.

OVERVIEW OF TABLE A

     C-1. Table A, austere qualification, is conducted from the crew’s vehicle and uses full
     caliber training ammunition on an authorized live-fire facility or safety certified training
     area. Table A trains and evaluate the crew’s ability to engage stationary or moving
     targets placed in a tactical array, during day and limited visibility conditions, with an
     operational or simulated degraded fire control system, as applicable. Crews execute
     these tasks from the offense and defense using full-caliber ammunition with scaled
     targetry at half-scale distances.

     C-2. This table requires each crew to successfully complete the live-fire prerequisites,
     all screening and zeroing procedures for their platform. The crew may complete this
     course without any additional live-fire training at an increased risk.

                  Requires General Officer Authorization

          Table A is an alternate means of qualification for heavy tank crews
          while deployed where no Army-standard range facilities are
          available. The first general officer in the chain of command
          authorizes its use.

     C-3. The planning and preparation process, including the uniform, facilities, and range
     support requirements are the same as Table VI, Qualification. Refer to chapter 6 for
     detailed information for those processes.

31 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-120  383

     C-5. They are specific to the half-scale range (range to target) requirements to ensure

     Units may not use North Atlantic Treaty Organization standard targets in lieu of the

     C-8. The tank commander engagement fired from their position using the main gun at

     engagement while simultaneously defeating multiple threats. The ranges to target listed
     are scaled and require no adjustment.
